In the runs behind this page, on an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU, the two boxes overlap almost completely, the medians land within about 10% of each other, and which one is faster changes from run to run. Treat the output above as one example: your hardware will give different numbers, so measure on the hardware you serve on. There is little to gain because the graph compiler already fuses elementwise operations into their neighbors: the kernel trace in Section 4.4 shows silu and the multiply applied in the epilogue of a matmul, so the plain version never writes the activation to memory either. Most of the time goes to the three matrix multiplications.

Write a custom op when MAX doesn’t have the operation you need, or when a better algorithm needs control that a chain of separate operations can’t express. Flash attention (Section 1.5) is the classic case: it never materializes the attention matrix, which no combination of separate operations can avoid. Elementwise ops written as above stay fusible, so the compiler can still merge them with their neighbors, and the same kernel runs on a GPU with no changes.

Kernels that need control over threads and memory, such as a matmul or attention, implement an execute method instead, written with Mojo’s GPU programming model. MAX’s guide to building custom ops shows the pattern, and the Mojo GPU puzzles teach GPU programming in Mojo from first principles. The handbook’s kernel optimization tools page compares Mojo with CUDA and Triton, and GPU architecture fundamentals explains what a kernel has to work with.

Exercises

  1. Write a gelu_tanh custom op as an ElementwiseUnaryOp (unary elementwise ops take one input) and test it against F.gelu with approximate="tanh".
  2. Add a float16 case to the tests. How large is the error, and why?
  3. Fuse more: write an op that takes gate, up, and a scale, and returns silu(gate) * up * scale. Does the timing change?

Next

Section 3.2 looks underneath compile: the graph MAX builds from your module, and the graph API you can use to build one directly.

3.2 Under the hood: the graph API

Since Section 1.2 you have called compile on modules and gotten a fast callable back. This section opens that box. compile traces your module into a graph, a complete description of the computation, and MAX’s compiler turns the graph into optimized code. You can also build graphs directly with max.graph, which is how many of MAX’s serving pipelines build their models, and load checkpoints with MAX’s production weight loader.

Show the setup 25 lines 
import logging
import re
from pathlib import Path

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from huggingface_hub import hf_hub_download
from max.driver import CPU, Buffer
from max.dtype import DType
from max.engine import InferenceSession
from max.graph import DeviceRef, Graph, TensorType, Weight, ops
from max.graph.weights import load_weights
from max.tensor import Tensor, default_device, default_dtype

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import MLP
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint, select

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
weights = load_checkpoint()

What compile builds

trace runs the first half of compile: it calls forward with symbolic inputs and records every operation into a graph, without compiling it. Here is SmolLM2’s MLP from Section 1.4:

with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    mlp = MLP(config)
mlp.load_state_dict(select(weights, "model.layers.0.mlp."))

staged = mlp.trace(
    TensorType(
        DType.float32,
        ("seq_len", config.hidden_size),
        device=DeviceRef.from_device(DEVICE),
    )
)
print("\n".join(str(staged.graph).splitlines()[:12]))

mo.graph @MLP<seq_len>(%arg0: !mo.tensor<[seq_len, 576], f32, gpu:0>) -> !mo.tensor<[seq_len, 576], f32, gpu:0> attributes {_kernel_library_paths = [], argument_names = ["input0"], result_names = ["output0"]} {
  %0 = mo.chain.create()
  %1 = mo.constant.external [name = "gate_proj.weight", align = 1, device = #M.device_ref<"cpu", 0>] : !mo.tensor<[1536, 576], f32>
  %result, %outChain = mo.transfer[%0] %1 : !mo.tensor<[1536, 576], f32> to <"gpu", 0>
  %2 = mo.constant <#M.dense_array<1, 0> : tensor<2xsi64>> : !mo.tensor<[2], si64>
  %3 = mo.transpose(%result, %2) : (!mo.tensor<[1536, 576], f32, gpu:0>, !mo.tensor<[2], si64>) -> !mo.tensor<[576, 1536], f32, gpu:0>
  %4 = rmo.matmul(%arg0, %3) : (!mo.tensor<[seq_len, 576], f32, gpu:0>, !mo.tensor<[576, 1536], f32, gpu:0>) -> !mo.tensor<[seq_len, 1536], f32, gpu:0>
  %5 = mo.constant <#M.dense_array<0.000000e+00> : tensor<f32>> : !mo.tensor<[], f32, gpu:0>
  %6 = rmo.add(%4, %5) : (!mo.tensor<[seq_len, 1536], f32, gpu:0>, !mo.tensor<[], f32, gpu:0>) -> !mo.tensor<[seq_len, 1536], f32, gpu:0>
  %7 = mo.silu(%6) : !mo.tensor<[seq_len, 1536], f32, gpu:0>
  %8 = mo.constant.external [name = "up_proj.weight", align = 1, device = #M.device_ref<"cpu", 0>] : !mo.tensor<[1536, 576], f32>
  %result_0, %outChain_1 = mo.transfer[%0] %8 : !mo.tensor<[1536, 576], f32> to <"gpu", 0>

The text is a dataflow graph: each line defines one value (%4) from earlier ones. Parse it and draw it, with each operation placed one column after the latest value it reads. Hover over a node to see its line of IR:

Show kind 85 lines 
LINE = re.compile(
    r"^\s*(%\w+)(?:, %\w+)* = ([\w.]+)(.*)$"
)  # some ops have two results
nodes, edges = {"%arg0": ("input", "input0: [seq_len, 576]")}, []
for line in str(staged.graph).splitlines():
    match = LINE.match(line)
    if not match or match.group(2) in ("mo.chain.create", "mo.constant"):
        continue  # skip bookkeeping and small constants
    value, op, rest = match.groups()
    weight = re.search(r'name = "([^"]+)"', rest)
    label = weight.group(1) if weight else op.split(".")[-1]
    nodes[value] = (label, line.strip())
    edges += [(src, value) for src in re.findall(r"%\w+", rest) if src in nodes]
output = re.search(r"mo.output (%\w+)", str(staged.graph)).group(1)
nodes["output"] = ("output", "output0: [seq_len, 576]")
edges.append((output, "output"))

depth = {}
for value in nodes:  # lines are in dataflow order
    depth[value] = max(
        (depth[src] + 1 for src, dst in edges if dst == value), default=0
    )
column = {}
position = {}
for value in nodes:
    row = column.get(depth[value], 0)
    column[depth[value]] = row + 1
    position[value] = (depth[value], -row)


def kind(label: str) -> str:
    if label in ("input", "output"):
        return "input or output"
    if label.endswith(".weight"):
        return "weight"
    if label == "transfer":
        return "copy to device"
    return "matmul" if label == "matmul" else "other operation"


colors = {
    "input or output": viz.VIOLET,
    "weight": viz.ORANGE,
    "copy to device": viz.YELLOW,
    "matmul": viz.BLUE,
    "other operation": viz.AQUA,
}
edge_x, edge_y = [], []
for src, dst in edges:
    edge_x += [position[src][0], position[dst][0], None]
    edge_y += [position[src][1], position[dst][1], None]
fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=edge_x,
        y=edge_y,
        mode="lines",
        line=dict(color=viz.GRID, width=2),
        hoverinfo="skip",
        showlegend=False,
    )
)
for name, color in colors.items():
    members = [v for v in nodes if kind(nodes[v][0]) == name]
    fig.add_trace(
        go.Scatter(
            x=[position[v][0] for v in members],
            y=[position[v][1] for v in members],
            mode="markers+text",
            name=name,
            text=[nodes[v][0] for v in members],
            textposition="top center",
            marker=dict(
                size=18, color=color, line=dict(color=viz.SURFACE, width=2)
            ),
            customdata=[nodes[v][1][:110] for v in members],
            hovertemplate="%{customdata}<extra></extra>",
        )
    )
fig.update_layout(
    title="The MLP's graph, as compile sees it",
    height=480,
    xaxis=dict(visible=False, range=[-0.8, max(depth.values()) + 0.6]),
    yaxis=dict(visible=False, range=[-max(column.values()) + 0.5, 0.6]),
)
viz.show(fig)

The graph is MAX’s intermediate representation (in MLIR). A few things to read in it:

  • The input type is [seq_len, 576]: seq_len stays symbolic, which is why one compiled graph serves every sequence length.
  • Each weight is a mo.constant.external with a name such as gate_proj.weight. The graph refers to weights by name and gets their values when it is loaded, so the same graph works with any checkpoint.
  • On a GPU, each weight also passes through a mo.transfer that copies it from host memory to the device (yellow in the diagram); on a CPU there is nothing to copy.
  • Each projection’s rmo.matmul is followed by an rmo.add of a constant 0.0. MAX’s Linear built with bias=False stores its bias as the number 0 and still adds it. It costs nothing at run time: the kernel trace in Section 4.4 shows no separate add.
  • Every line is one operation (rmo.matmul, mo.silu, …) with fully known types. That is what lets the compiler fuse operations, plan memory for the whole computation, and generate code for the target device ahead of time.

Build a graph yourself

max.graph builds graphs directly. A Graph takes a name, the input types, and a forward function that combines max.graph.ops operations. Weights are declared as Weight objects with a name, dtype, shape, and device:

H, I = config.hidden_size, config.intermediate_size


def mlp_forward(x):
    def weight(name: str, shape: list[int]) -> Weight:
        return Weight(
            name, DType.float32, shape, device=DeviceRef.from_device(DEVICE)
        )

    gate = x @ weight("gate_proj.weight", [I, H]).T
    up = x @ weight("up_proj.weight", [I, H]).T
    return (ops.silu(gate) * up) @ weight("down_proj.weight", [H, I]).T


graph = Graph(
    "mlp",
    forward=mlp_forward,
    input_types=[
        TensorType(
            DType.float32, ("seq_len", H), device=DeviceRef.from_device(DEVICE)
        )
    ],
)

An InferenceSession compiles graphs for a set of devices. load compiles this one and binds each named weight from a registry; execute runs it on Buffers, the driver’s plain device memory:

session = InferenceSession(devices=[DEVICE])
compiled_mlp = session.load(
    graph, weights_registry=select(weights, "model.layers.0.mlp.")
)

x = np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal((5, H)).astype(np.float32)
(out,) = compiled_mlp.execute(Buffer.from_numpy(x).to(DEVICE))
expected = mlp(Tensor(x, device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()
scale = np.abs(expected).max()
relative = np.abs(out.to_numpy() - expected).max() / scale
print(
    f"max difference from your module, relative to the largest output: {relative:.1e}"
)
assert relative < 1e-5

max difference from your module, relative to the largest output: 4.8e-07

The hand-built graph and your module agree because Module.compile builds this same graph for you, loads it in a session shared by the whole process, and wraps the result so it takes and returns Tensors.

Custom ops work in graphs too, through ops.custom. Compile the kernel from Section 3.1 again and use it in place of silu * up:

import mojo.notebook
Show SwiGLU 14 lines 
%%mojo package -o kernels.mojoc
import extensibility
from extensibility import ElementwiseBinaryOp
from std.math import exp


@extensibility.register("swiglu")
struct SwiGLU(ElementwiseBinaryOp):
    @staticmethod
    def elementwise[
        dtype: DType, width: SIMDLength
    ](gate: SIMD[dtype, width], up: SIMD[dtype, width]) -> SIMD[dtype, width]:
        comptime assert dtype.is_floating_point(), "SwiGLU needs floats"
        return gate / (1 + exp(-gate)) * up
Show fused_forward 34 lines 
def fused_forward(x):
    def weight(name: str, shape: list[int]) -> Weight:
        return Weight(
            name, DType.float32, shape, device=DeviceRef.from_device(DEVICE)
        )

    gate = x @ weight("gate_proj.weight", [I, H]).T
    up = x @ weight("up_proj.weight", [I, H]).T
    (h,) = ops.custom(
        "swiglu",
        device=DeviceRef.from_device(DEVICE),
        values=[gate, up],
        out_types=[gate.type],
    )
    return h.tensor @ weight("down_proj.weight", [H, I]).T


fused_graph = Graph(
    "fused_mlp",
    forward=fused_forward,
    input_types=[
        TensorType(
            DType.float32, ("seq_len", H), device=DeviceRef.from_device(DEVICE)
        )
    ],
    custom_extensions=[Path("kernels.mojoc")],
)
fused_mlp = session.load(
    fused_graph, weights_registry=select(weights, "model.layers.0.mlp.")
)
(fused_out,) = fused_mlp.execute(Buffer.from_numpy(x).to(DEVICE))
relative = np.abs(fused_out.to_numpy() - expected).max() / scale
print(f"max difference, relative to the largest output: {relative:.1e}")
assert relative < 1e-5

max difference, relative to the largest output: 4.8e-07

Production weight loading

The loader from Section 1.3 reads one safetensors file into memory as float32. MAX’s own loader, max.graph.weights.load_weights, reads safetensors (including checkpoints split across many files) and GGUF. It maps files into memory instead of reading them, so a tensor is read only when it’s used, and it keeps each tensor’s original dtype and, for GGUF, its quantization encoding. Tensors are reached by attribute path:

path = Path(hf_hub_download(MODEL_ID, "model.safetensors"))
checkpoint = load_weights([path])
gate = checkpoint.model.layers[0].mlp.gate_proj.weight
print(gate.name)
weight = gate.allocate()
print(weight.shape, weight.dtype)

model.layers.0.mlp.gate_proj.weight
[Dim(1536), Dim(576)] DType.bfloat16

allocate returns a Weight like the ones in your hand-built graph, with the checkpoint’s name, shape, and dtype filled in. A serving pipeline builds its graph from these, so the graph matches the checkpoint exactly, and the loader supplies the data when the graph is loaded.

Why it matters

Because the whole computation is known before it runs, MAX can:

  • fuse operations and plan memory across the entire model instead of one operation at a time;
  • compile once, ahead of time, and cache the result (the reason max serve starts faster the second time);
  • on GPUs, record a decode step’s kernel launches and replay them with almost no CPU overhead (device graph capture);
  • run the same graph on CPUs and on NVIDIA, AMD, and Apple GPUs.

max.nn modules with compile give you all of this while you write ordinary Python. Use max.graph directly when you need control over exactly what goes into a graph, as MAX’s serving pipelines do. MAX’s docs describe the graph API in full; the handbook’s kernel optimization page explains why whole-graph compilation and fusion matter for LLMs.

  2. Build the MLP graph with a static input shape, [5, 576], instead of seq_len. What happens when you execute it with 6 positions?
  3. Load layer 0’s MLP weights through load_weights instead of load_checkpoint. What has to change for the float32 graph?

4. Testing and benchmarking

A model has to be correct before it is worth making fast. This chapter applies the tools MAX uses to test and measure its own models to yours.

4.1 Testing layers against references

Chapter 1 checked each layer once, on one input. Real tests check many shapes against an independent reference, with metrics that tell a rounding difference from a bug. This section builds the pattern MAX uses for its own layers, in max/python/layer_benchmarks, and applies it to your decoder layer, with Hugging Face’s PyTorch Llama as the reference.

Show the setup 29 lines 
import logging
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
import torch
from max import functional as F
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.graph import TensorType
from max.tensor import Tensor, default_device, default_dtype
from transformers import AutoConfig
from transformers.models.llama.modeling_llama import (
    LlamaDecoderLayer,
    LlamaRotaryEmbedding,
)

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint, select

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
layer0 = select(load_checkpoint(), "model.layers.0.")

How to compare outputs

One number is not enough. MAX’s test harness reports three:

  • The maximum absolute error catches any single element that is far off.
  • The mean absolute error is the typical error, which tells a few outliers from a systematic drift.
  • The cosine distance, \(1 - \cos\theta\) between the flattened outputs, is near 0 when the outputs point the same way even if their scale differs slightly, and large for a structural bug such as a wrong head layout.

A test passes when the outputs are allclose, within an absolute and a relative tolerance, and the cosine distance is below a threshold. The defaults below, 1e-3 for both tolerances and 1e-5 for the cosine distance, are tighter than MAX’s (1e-2 and 1e-3) because every test here runs in float32; MAX’s bfloat16 layer tests loosen them further.

Show CorrectnessResult, compare_outputs 48 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

from dataclasses import dataclass

import numpy as np


@dataclass
class CorrectnessResult:
    """How far an output is from its reference, as MAX's layer tests report it."""

    label: str
    mae: float
    max_abs_err: float
    cos_dist: float
    passed: bool

    def __str__(self) -> str:
        status = "pass" if self.passed else "FAIL"
        return (
            f"{self.label:16} mae {self.mae:.1e}  max {self.max_abs_err:.1e}  "
            f"cos dist {self.cos_dist:.1e}  {status}"
        )


def compare_outputs(
    actual: np.ndarray,
    expected: np.ndarray,
    label: str = "",
    atol: float = 1e-3,
    rtol: float = 1e-3,
    cos_threshold: float = 1e-5,
) -> CorrectnessResult:
    """Compares ``actual`` with ``expected``; passes when they are allclose
    and their cosine distance is below ``cos_threshold``."""
    a = actual.astype(np.float64).ravel()
    e = expected.astype(np.float64).ravel()
    cos_dist = 1.0 - float(
        a @ e / (np.linalg.norm(a) * np.linalg.norm(e) + 1e-12)
    )
    allclose = bool(np.allclose(actual, expected, atol=atol, rtol=rtol))
    return CorrectnessResult(
        label=label,
        mae=float(np.mean(np.abs(a - e))),
        max_abs_err=float(np.max(np.abs(a - e))),
        cos_dist=cos_dist,
        passed=allclose and cos_dist < cos_threshold,
    )

Saved to minillm/testing.py.

The reference

Hugging Face’s LlamaDecoderLayer is an independent implementation of the same layer, in PyTorch. Its parameter names match yours, so the same checkpoint entries load into both. "eager" selects its plain attention code instead of a fused kernel, so the reference stays simple.

hf_config = AutoConfig.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID, attn_implementation="eager")
reference = LlamaDecoderLayer(hf_config, layer_idx=0).float().eval()
reference.load_state_dict(
    {name: torch.from_numpy(w.copy()) for name, w in layer0.items()}
)
rotary = LlamaRotaryEmbedding(hf_config)


def reference_layer(x: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    batch, seq, _ = x.shape
    hidden = torch.from_numpy(x)
    positions = torch.arange(seq).expand(batch, seq)
    mask = torch.full((seq, seq), float("-inf")).triu(1)[None, None]
    with torch.no_grad():
        out = reference(
            hidden,
            attention_mask=mask,
            position_ids=positions,
            position_embeddings=rotary(hidden, positions),
        )
    return out.numpy()

Compile once, test many shapes

MAX’s harness separates static parameters, which fix the graph (here, the layer’s sizes), from dynamic ones, which vary per run (batch size and sequence length). Compile once with symbolic dimensions, then run every shape through the same compiled layer. The shapes cover the edges: a single token (a decode step), odd lengths, a batch, and a long prefill.

Show compiled_layer, check 34 lines 
def compiled_layer(layer_class=DecoderLayer, weights=layer0, cfg=config):
    with F.lazy(), default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
        layer = layer_class(cfg)
    layer.load_state_dict(weights)
    return layer.compile(
        TensorType(
            DType.float32, ("batch", "seq_len", cfg.hidden_size), device=DEVICE
        )
    )


SHAPES = [(1, 1), (1, 7), (2, 33), (1, 512)]


def check(run, shapes=SHAPES) -> list:
    rng = np.random.default_rng(0)
    results = []
    for batch, seq in shapes:
        x = rng.standard_normal((batch, seq, config.hidden_size)).astype(
            np.float32
        )
        actual = run(Tensor(x, device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()
        results.append(
            compare_outputs(
                actual, reference_layer(x), label=f"batch {batch}, seq {seq}"
            )
        )
    return results


correct = check(compiled_layer())
for result in correct:
    print(result)
assert all(r.passed for r in correct)

batch 1, seq 1   mae 2.7e-07  max 2.9e-06  cos dist 2.4e-14  pass
batch 1, seq 7   mae 6.5e-07  max 1.7e-05  cos dist 1.9e-13  pass
batch 2, seq 33  mae 7.0e-07  max 2.1e-05  cos dist 1.9e-13  pass
batch 1, seq 512 mae 7.1e-07  max 3.8e-05  cos dist 2.0e-13  pass

Every shape passes, with maximum errors of 4e-5 or less and cosine distances near zero.

A test must be able to fail

A test that never fails proves nothing, so check that this one catches a real bug. A plausible one is a config read wrong, with the base of RoPE (Section 1.4) at Llama 2’s 10000 instead of SmolLM2’s 100000:

from dataclasses import replace

buggy = compiled_layer(cfg=replace(config, rope_theta=10000.0))
buggy_results = check(buggy)
for result in buggy_results:
    print(result)
assert not all(r.passed for r in buggy_results)

batch 1, seq 1   mae 2.7e-07  max 2.9e-06  cos dist 2.4e-14  pass
batch 1, seq 7   mae 4.9e-04  max 6.6e-03  cos dist 1.2e-07  FAIL
batch 2, seq 33  mae 2.2e-03  max 1.3e-01  cos dist 2.3e-06  FAIL
batch 1, seq 512 mae 5.0e-03  max 2.3e-01  cos dist 1.1e-05  FAIL
Show the code 45 lines 
ATOL, COS_THRESHOLD = 1e-3, 1e-5
fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=[1e-7, ATOL, ATOL, 1e-7],
        y=[1e-15, 1e-15, COS_THRESHOLD, COS_THRESHOLD],
        fill="toself",
        fillcolor="rgba(27,175,122,0.15)",
        line=dict(color=viz.AQUA, width=1, dash="dot"),
        name="pass region (about)",
        hoverinfo="skip",
    )
)
for name, results, color in [
    ("correct layer", correct, viz.BLUE),
    ("rope_theta = 10000", buggy_results, viz.ORANGE),
]:
    fig.add_trace(
        go.Scatter(
            x=[r.max_abs_err for r in results],
            y=[max(r.cos_dist, 1e-15) for r in results],
            mode="markers+text",
            name=name,
            text=[
                r.label.replace("batch ", "b").replace(", seq ", "/s")
                for r in results
            ],
            textposition="middle right",
            marker=dict(
                color=color,
                size=11,
                symbol=["circle" if r.passed else "x" for r in results],
            ),
            hovertemplate="%{text}: max error %{x:.1e}, cos dist %{y:.1e}"
            + "<extra>"
            + name
            + "</extra>",
        )
    )
fig.update_layout(
    title="Each shape's max error against its cosine distance",
    height=440,
    xaxis=dict(title="max |error| (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
    yaxis=dict(title="cosine distance (log scale)", type="log", dtick=2),
)
viz.show(fig)

Each point is one shape, labeled batch/sequence length, and an x marks a failure. The shaded corner is where both checks pass; its right edge is only approximate, because allclose also allows a relative error of 1e-3 of each expected value.

The single-token case still passes: at position 0, RoPE doesn’t rotate at all, so the bug can’t show there. Every longer input fails. The maximum error grows by two to four orders of magnitude and breaks the allclose check. The cosine distance grows by six to eight orders of magnitude, but it crosses its 1e-5 threshold only on the 512-token input. The two checks catch different bugs, which is why the test uses both, and why the shape list must include sequences, not just one token.

Where in the sequence does the bug show? Run the 512-token input through both layers and plot the largest error at each position:

x = (
    np.random.default_rng(1)
    .standard_normal((1, 512, config.hidden_size))
    .astype(np.float32)
)
expected = reference_layer(x)[0]
per_position = {
    name: np.abs(run(Tensor(x, device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()[0] - expected).max(-1)
    for name, run in [
        ("correct layer", compiled_layer()),
        ("rope_theta = 10000", buggy),
    ]
}
Show the code 22 lines 
fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=np.arange(512),
            y=np.maximum(err, 1e-9),
            name=name,
            mode="lines",
            line_color=color,
            hovertemplate="position %{x}: %{y:.1e}<extra>" + name + "</extra>",
        )
        for (name, err), color in zip(
            per_position.items(), [viz.BLUE, viz.ORANGE]
        )
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Largest error at each position of a 512-token input",
    height=400,
    xaxis_title="position",
    yaxis=dict(title="max |error| (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
)
viz.show(fig)

At position 0 the two layers agree, because RoPE doesn’t rotate there. Within a few positions the buggy layer’s error is three to four orders of magnitude larger, and it stays there for the whole sequence, while the correct layer’s error stays near 1e-5. A test that only ran one token would have passed both.

As a pytest file

In a repository, these checks belong in a test file that runs on every change. %%writefile saves a cell as a file. The test compiles the layer once per module, as MAX’s own tests do, and checks every shape:

Show the code 45 lines 
%%writefile test_decoder_layer.py
import numpy as np
import pytest
import torch
from max.dtype import DType
from max.graph import TensorType
from max.tensor import Tensor, default_device, default_dtype
from transformers import AutoConfig
from transformers.models.llama.modeling_llama import LlamaDecoderLayer, LlamaRotaryEmbedding

from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer
from minillm.testing import compare_outputs
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint, select

CONFIG = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()


@pytest.fixture(scope="module")
def layers():
    weights = select(load_checkpoint(), "model.layers.0.")
    with default_device(pick_device()), default_dtype(DType.float32):
        ours = DecoderLayer(CONFIG)
    ours.load_state_dict(weights)
    compiled = ours.compile(
        TensorType(DType.float32, ("batch", "seq_len", CONFIG.hidden_size), device=pick_device())
    )
    hf_config = AutoConfig.from_pretrained(MODEL_ID, attn_implementation="eager")
    reference = LlamaDecoderLayer(hf_config, layer_idx=0).float().eval()
    reference.load_state_dict({k: torch.from_numpy(v.copy()) for k, v in weights.items()})
    return compiled, reference, LlamaRotaryEmbedding(hf_config)


@pytest.mark.parametrize("batch, seq", [(1, 1), (1, 7), (2, 33)])
def test_decoder_layer_matches_reference(layers, batch, seq):
    compiled, reference, rotary = layers
    x = np.random.default_rng(seq).standard_normal((batch, seq, CONFIG.hidden_size)).astype(np.float32)
    hidden, positions = torch.from_numpy(x), torch.arange(seq).expand(batch, seq)
    mask = torch.full((seq, seq), float("-inf")).triu(1)[None, None]
    with torch.no_grad():
        expected = reference(hidden, attention_mask=mask, position_ids=positions,
                             position_embeddings=rotary(hidden, positions)).numpy()
    result = compare_outputs(compiled(Tensor(x, device=pick_device())).to_numpy(), expected)
    assert result.passed, result

...                                                                      [100%]
3 passed in 4.76s

MAX’s own layer tests follow the same shape, with one more step: each harness also benchmarks the layer on the same shapes, so correctness and speed are measured together. Section 4.3 covers benchmarking.

Next

Section 4.2 tests the whole model: its logits on real prompts, against the same reference.

4.2 Verifying a model’s logits

Layer tests catch bugs in one layer. A whole model can still go wrong in ways no layer test sees: a weight loaded into the wrong layer, a tied head that isn’t tied, a mask that only fails on long prompts. So model bring-ups end with logit verification: run the same prompts through your model and a trusted reference, and compare the logits at every position.

This section verifies your SmolLM2 against Hugging Face’s PyTorch implementation, with the metrics MAX’s own verification tools use.

Show the setup 36 lines 
import logging
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
import torch
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.graph import TensorType
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer
from max.tensor import Tensor
from transformers import AutoModelForCausalLM

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.kv_cache import CachedLlamaForCausalLM, KVCacheDecoder
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.sampling import generate
from minillm.testing import compare_outputs
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
weights = load_checkpoint()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)

ours = load_model(config, weights, DEVICE).compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)
reference = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    MODEL_ID, torch_dtype=torch.float32
).eval()

Prompts

Verification prompts should exercise different behavior: plain prose, code, numbers, another language, and a prompt long enough to stress attention and RoPE at larger positions.

long_prompt = " ".join(["The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog."] * 60)
prompts = {
    "prose": "The capital of France is Paris, and the capital of Italy is",
    "code": "def fibonacci(n):\n    if n < 2:\n        return n\n    return",
    "numbers": "1, 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, 21,",
    "french": "Le chat est assis sur",
    "long": long_prompt,
}


def logits_pair(text: str) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
    ids = tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0]
    mine = ours(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()[0]
    with torch.no_grad():
        theirs = reference(torch.tensor([ids])).logits[0].numpy()
    return mine, theirs

Metrics for logits

Logits feed a softmax, so on top of the elementwise metrics from Section 4.1, two measures matter for generation.

The KL divergence between the next-token distributions measures how much probability mass moved. It is the standard measure for comparing two models’ predictions, and it ignores differences in logits that softmax removes, such as a constant offset. For the reference distribution \(P\) and yours, \(Q\), at one position:

\[D_{\mathrm{KL}}(P \,\Vert\, Q) = \sum_i P_i \log \frac{P_i}{Q_i}\]

It is 0 only when the two distributions are identical.

Top-1 agreement is the fraction of positions at which both models predict the same most likely token. It is all that greedy decoding sees.

Show softmax, kl_divergence 26 lines 
def softmax(z: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    z = z.astype(np.float64) - z.max(-1, keepdims=True)
    e = np.exp(z)
    return e / e.sum(-1, keepdims=True)


def kl_divergence(p_logits: np.ndarray, q_logits: np.ndarray) -> float:
    """Mean KL(P || Q) over positions, in nats."""
    p, q = softmax(p_logits), softmax(q_logits)
    return float((p * (np.log(p + 1e-30) - np.log(q + 1e-30))).sum(-1).mean())


print(
    f"{'prompt':8} {'positions':>9} {'max err':>8} {'cos dist':>9} {'KL':>9} {'top-1':>6}"
)
errors = {}
for name, text in prompts.items():
    mine, theirs = logits_pair(text)
    errors[name] = np.abs(mine - theirs).max(-1)
    r = compare_outputs(mine, theirs, atol=1e-3, rtol=1e-3)
    top1 = (mine.argmax(-1) == theirs.argmax(-1)).mean()
    print(
        f"{name:8} {len(mine):>9} {r.max_abs_err:>8.1e} {r.cos_dist:>9.1e} "
        f"{kl_divergence(theirs, mine):>9.1e} {top1:>6.0%}"
    )
    assert top1 >= 0.99 and kl_divergence(theirs, mine) < 1e-6

prompt   positions  max err  cos dist        KL  top-1
prose           13  8.2e-05   2.4e-12   3.2e-11   100%
code            18  8.3e-05   1.5e-12   4.0e-11   100%
numbers         25  1.1e-04   2.1e-12   2.2e-11   100%
french           6  1.0e-04   1.3e-12   8.6e-11   100%
long           600  1.1e-04   2.6e-12   2.5e-12   100%

A summary number can hide a trend. Plot the largest logit error at each position: if a bug in RoPE or the mask grew with position, the long prompt would show it as a rising line.

Show the code 22 lines 
fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=np.arange(1, len(err) + 1),
            y=err,
            customdata=np.arange(len(err)),
            name=name,
            mode="lines",
            line_color=color,
            hovertemplate=name
            + ", position %{customdata}: %{y:.1e}<extra></extra>",
        )
        for (name, err), color in zip(errors.items(), viz.SERIES)
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Largest logit error at each position, against Hugging Face",
    height=400,
    xaxis=dict(title="position + 1 (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
    yaxis=dict(title="max |logit difference|", type="log", dtick=1),
)
viz.show(fig)

Every prompt agrees at every position, including the 600-token one, with 100% top-1 agreement and KL divergences between about 1e-12 and 1e-10 nats. MAX verifies its own models the same way, comparing MAX’s logits with reference logits from PyTorch or vLLM by cosine distance, KL divergence, and tolerances. The model bring-up workflow guide covers the rest of bringing a new architecture to MAX.

Greedy generation

Matching logits at the prompt is necessary but not sufficient: generation feeds the model its own outputs, through your KV cache from Section 1.8. Compare 48 greedy tokens from your cached decoder with Hugging Face’s generate:

Show the code 27 lines 
cached = KVCacheDecoder(
    load_model(config, weights, DEVICE, CachedLlamaForCausalLM), config, DEVICE
)

gaps = {}
for name in ["prose", "code", "numbers"]:
    ids = tokenizer([prompts[name]])["input_ids"][0]
    mine = generate(cached, ids, 48, temperature=0)
    with torch.no_grad():
        out = reference.generate(
            torch.tensor([ids]),
            max_new_tokens=48,
            do_sample=False,
            output_logits=True,
            return_dict_in_generate=True,
        )
    theirs = out.sequences[0, len(ids) :].tolist()
    gaps[name] = [
        float(np.diff(np.sort(step[0].numpy())[-2:])[0]) for step in out.logits
    ]
    same = next(
        (i for i, (a, b) in enumerate(zip(mine, theirs)) if a != b), len(mine)
    )
    print(f"{name:8} identical for {same} of {len(mine)} tokens")
    assert same >= 24, (
        "diverged early; check the gap between the top two logits"
    )

prose    identical for 48 of 48 tokens
code     identical for 48 of 48 tokens
numbers  identical for 48 of 48 tokens

At each greedy step, how far ahead was the winning token? output_logits=True returns the reference’s raw logits at every step; plot the gap between the top two, with the largest logit error from the first chart as a dotted line:

Show the code 32 lines 
fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=np.arange(len(gap)),
            y=gap,
            name=name,
            mode="lines+markers",
            line_color=color,
            marker=dict(size=5),
            hovertemplate=name + ", step %{x}: gap %{y:.3f}<extra></extra>",
        )
        for (name, gap), color in zip(gaps.items(), viz.SERIES)
    ]
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Gap between the top two logits at each greedy step",
    height=400,
    xaxis_title="generated token",
    yaxis=dict(title="logit gap (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1),
)
largest_error = max(err.max() for err in errors.values())
fig.add_trace(
    go.Scatter(
        x=[0, max(len(gap) for gap in gaps.values()) - 1],
        y=[largest_error] * 2,
        name=f"largest logit error ({largest_error:.0e})",
        mode="lines",
        line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dot", width=1),
        hoverinfo="skip",
    )
)
viz.show(fig)

A rounding difference can change the winner only at a step where the gap is smaller than the error, that is, where a line dips below the dotted one. Here the smallest gap is above 0.1 and the largest error about 1e-4, so no step comes close.

If a run does diverge after many tokens, the cause is usually a near tie: at some step two tokens have almost the same logit, and a difference in the fourth or fifth decimal place, from summing in a different order, picks a different winner. From then on the two texts differ, though both are valid greedy continuations. When you see a divergence, look at the gap between the top two logits at that step: a tiny gap means rounding, a large one means a bug.

Beyond logits: accuracy evaluations

Logit verification compares two implementations. It can’t tell you whether the model is good at a task, or whether a change that alters numbers on purpose, such as quantization, costs accuracy. For that, serving teams run task evaluations such as GSM8K or MMLU against the server (Section 7.1 serves your model). The handbook’s quantization page covers how quantized models are evaluated.

Exercises

  1. Run the comparison in bfloat16 (cast the weights and cast the logits back to float32). How do KL divergence and top-1 agreement change?
  2. Find the step where two tokens’ logits are closest during a greedy run. How small is the gap?
  3. Load one layer’s weights into the wrong layer index and rerun the table. Which prompt and metric show it most clearly?

Next

Section 4.3 measures speed: how to benchmark kernels and layers so the numbers mean something.

4.3 Benchmarking kernels and layers

A benchmark is an experiment, and most quick timings are bad experiments: they include compilation, measure the time to launch work instead of the time to finish it, or report one noisy sample. This section writes a small benchmark harness that avoids those mistakes, then uses it to show why decoding one token is limited by memory traffic and prefill by arithmetic.

Show the setup 23 lines 
import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.driver import CPU
from max.dtype import DType
from max.graph import TensorType
from max.nn import Linear
from max.tensor import Tensor, default_device, default_dtype
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint, select

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU
config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()

A harness

The harness follows four rules, in the spirit of MAX’s layer benchmarks, which time on the device with events and report the median and p95:

  1. Warm up. The first calls compile kernels and fill caches, so don’t time them.
  2. Wait for the result. Work can run asynchronously, especially on a GPU. Reading the output (to_numpy()) forces it to finish.
  3. Repeat. One sample is mostly noise, so take many.
  4. Report the median and a tail percentile. Outliers pull the mean; the median shows the typical run and p90 shows how slow an unlucky one gets.
Show Timing, benchmark 38 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

import time
from collections.abc import Callable
from dataclasses import dataclass

import numpy as np


@dataclass
class Timing:
    """Milliseconds per call over the measured iterations."""

    median_ms: float
    p90_ms: float
    min_ms: float

    def __str__(self) -> str:
        return f"median {self.median_ms:7.2f} ms  p90 {self.p90_ms:7.2f} ms"


def benchmark(
    fn: Callable[[], object], warmup: int = 3, iterations: int = 20
) -> Timing:
    """Times ``fn``, which must return a fully computed result (for example,
    a NumPy array read back from the device)."""
    for _ in range(warmup):
        fn()
    samples = []
    for _ in range(iterations):
        start = time.perf_counter()
        fn()
        samples.append((time.perf_counter() - start) * 1000)
    return Timing(
        median_ms=float(np.median(samples)),
        p90_ms=float(np.percentile(samples, 90)),
        min_ms=float(np.min(samples)),
    )

Saved to minillm/bench.py.

Memory-bound or compute-bound?

Every projection in the model is a matrix multiplication, x @ W.T, with x holding one row per token. Its cost has two parts: reading W from memory, which doesn’t depend on the number of tokens, and the arithmetic, which grows with it. For \(n\) tokens and a \(K \to N\) projection in float32 (4 bytes per number), the ratio of the two is the operation’s arithmetic intensity:

\[I = \frac{\text{FLOPs}}{\text{bytes moved}} = \frac{2nKN}{4\,(KN + nK + nN)}\]

A device can’t run faster than its compute peak \(P_{\text{peak}}\), nor faster than its memory bandwidth \(B\) can feed it, which gives the roofline:

\[P \le \min\left(P_{\text{peak}},\; B \cdot I\right)\]

With few tokens, \(I\) is small and memory sets the limit; with many, \(I\) grows until compute does. Measure SmolLM2’s gate_proj (\(K = 576\), \(N = 1536\)) for 1 to 2048 tokens:

Show the code 29 lines 
with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    proj = Linear(config.hidden_size, config.intermediate_size, bias=False)
run = proj.compile(
    TensorType(DType.float32, ("tokens", config.hidden_size), device=DEVICE)
)

K, N = config.hidden_size, config.intermediate_size
rows = []
for tokens in [1, 4, 16, 64, 256, 1024, 2048]:
    x = Tensor(
        np.random.default_rng(0)
        .standard_normal((tokens, K))
        .astype(np.float32),
        device=DEVICE,
    )
    t = benchmark(lambda: run(x).to_numpy())
    flops = 2 * tokens * K * N
    bytes_moved = 4 * (K * N + tokens * (K + N))
    rows.append(
        (
            tokens,
            t.median_ms,
            flops / t.median_ms / 1e6,
            bytes_moved / t.median_ms / 1e6,
        )
    )
    print(
        f"{tokens:5} tokens  {t}  {rows[-1][2]:7.1f} GFLOP/s  {rows[-1][3]:6.1f} GB/s"
    )

    1 tokens  median    0.56 ms  p90    0.63 ms      3.1 GFLOP/s     6.3 GB/s
    4 tokens  median    0.35 ms  p90    0.66 ms     20.3 GFLOP/s    10.2 GB/s
   16 tokens  median    0.39 ms  p90    0.47 ms     72.0 GFLOP/s     9.3 GB/s
   64 tokens  median    0.86 ms  p90    1.25 ms    131.0 GFLOP/s     4.7 GB/s
  256 tokens  median    2.07 ms  p90    2.97 ms    218.5 GFLOP/s     2.8 GB/s
 1024 tokens  median    8.22 ms  p90   11.20 ms    220.3 GFLOP/s     1.5 GB/s
 2048 tokens  median   10.09 ms  p90   14.24 ms    359.1 GFLOP/s     2.1 GB/s
Show the code 82 lines 
tokens, ms, gflops, gbps = zip(*rows)
intensity = [
    2 * t * K * N / (4 * (K * N + t * (K + N))) for t in tokens
]  # FLOP per byte moved

fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1,
    cols=2,
    horizontal_spacing=0.14,
    subplot_titles=["Time per call", "Arithmetic throughput against intensity"],
)
fig.add_trace(
    go.Scatter(
        x=tokens,
        y=ms,
        mode="lines+markers",
        line_color=viz.BLUE,
        showlegend=False,
        hovertemplate="%{x} tokens: %{y:.3f} ms<extra></extra>",
    ),
    row=1,
    col=1,
)
fig.add_trace(
    go.Scatter(
        x=intensity,
        y=gflops,
        mode="lines+markers+text",
        line_color=viz.ORANGE,
        text=[str(t) for t in tokens],
        textposition="top left",
        showlegend=False,
        customdata=tokens,
        hovertemplate="%{customdata} tokens<br>%{x:.1f} FLOP/byte<br>%{y:.0f} GFLOP/s<extra></extra>",
    ),
    row=1,
    col=2,
)
# The roof uses the best bandwidth and throughput this sweep reached, not the
# device's datasheet numbers, so every point sits on or under it.
peak, bandwidth = max(gflops), max(gbps)
roof_x = np.geomspace(min(intensity) / 2, max(intensity) * 2, 200)
fig.add_trace(
    go.Scatter(
        x=roof_x,
        y=np.minimum(peak, bandwidth * roof_x),
        mode="lines",
        line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dash", width=1.5),
        name="measured roofline",
        hovertemplate="roof at %{x:.1f} FLOP/byte: %{y:.0f} GFLOP/s<extra></extra>",
    ),
    row=1,
    col=2,
)
fig.add_annotation(
    x=np.log10(peak / bandwidth),
    y=np.log10(peak),
    text=f"ridge: {peak / bandwidth:.0f} FLOP/byte",
    showarrow=True,
    arrowhead=0,
    ay=-30,
    row=1,
    col=2,
)
fig.update_xaxes(title_text="tokens", type="log", dtick=1, row=1, col=1)
fig.update_yaxes(title_text="ms (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1, row=1, col=1)
fig.update_xaxes(
    title_text="FLOP per byte moved (log scale)",
    type="log",
    dtick=1,
    row=1,
    col=2,
)
fig.update_yaxes(
    title_text="GFLOP/s (log scale)", type="log", dtick=1, row=1, col=2
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="gate_proj (576 to 1536) from 1 to 2048 tokens; labels are token counts",
    height=420,
    legend=dict(x=0.58, y=1.12),
)
viz.show(fig)

Read the two plots together, and hover over the points. The dashed line on the right is the roofline drawn from this sweep: its slope is the best bandwidth any point reached, its flat top the best throughput. Points left of the ridge sit under the slanted, memory-bound part of the roof; points to the right sit under the flat, compute-bound part. A device’s datasheet ceilings are higher than these measured ones.

With few tokens, the time barely grows: 1 to 16 tokens all take about half a millisecond on this page’s machine, an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. Each weight is used for only a few multiply-adds after it is loaded, so arithmetic throughput is low and the operation is memory-bound. At a matrix this small, though, most of that time is a fixed cost per call, for launching the work and reading the result back: the single-token call moves W’s 3.5 MB at under 10 GB/s, far below the GPU’s memory bandwidth. In a large model, reading the weights dominates instead.

With many tokens, each weight, once loaded, is reused for every token, so arithmetic throughput climbs and the time grows with the number of tokens. The operation moves toward the compute-bound end, where the hardware’s arithmetic units set the limit.

Decoding one token per step runs every weight matrix at the memory-bound end. That is why per-token latency depends on memory bandwidth, and why serving engines batch many requests into each step (Chapter 5): here, 16 tokens cost barely more than one. Prefill processes the whole prompt at once and runs toward the compute-bound end. The handbook’s GPU memory page explains the same trade-off on GPUs.

Benchmark a layer

The same harness measures a whole decoder layer at the shapes that matter in serving: decode steps (one token) for several batch sizes, and prefills of several lengths.

with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    layer = DecoderLayer(config)
layer.load_state_dict(select(load_checkpoint(), "model.layers.0."))
run_layer = layer.compile(
    TensorType(
        DType.float32, ("batch", "seq_len", config.hidden_size), device=DEVICE
    )
)

print("shape (batch x seq)       time per call       tokens/s")
medians = {}
for batch, seq in [(1, 1), (8, 1), (32, 1), (1, 128), (1, 512)]:
    x = Tensor(
        np.random.default_rng(0)
        .standard_normal((batch, seq, config.hidden_size))
        .astype(np.float32),
        device=DEVICE,
    )
    t = benchmark(lambda: run_layer(x).to_numpy(), warmup=5, iterations=50)
    medians[batch, seq] = t.median_ms
    print(f"{batch:5} x {seq:<5}  {t}  {batch * seq / t.median_ms * 1000:9.0f}")
print(
    f"a decode step for 32 sequences costs {medians[32, 1] / medians[1, 1]:.1f}x one sequence's"
)

On this machine, a decode step for 32 sequences costs well under twice a single sequence’s step, so throughput in tokens per second rises sharply with batch size. Timings on a laptop are noisy, which is why the harness reports a percentile alongside the median. A 512-token prefill runs through one layer at tens of thousands of tokens per second; the model has 30 layers, so a whole-model prefill would run at a thirtieth of that, under 2,000 tokens per second.

These numbers describe this machine’s GPU. On another CPU or GPU the curves have the same shape at different scales, although the time isn’t always monotonic in size: the kernels behind each operation choose different strategies for different shapes, so on some devices a smaller batch takes longer than a larger one (this page’s run doesn’t show it). Measure the shapes you serve. MAX’s own layer benchmarks run this kind of sweep for every layer, and its kernel benchmarks do the same for individual kernels. For whole-server measurements, Section 7.2 uses max benchmark. The handbook’s LLM performance benchmarks page explains how to read published numbers.

  3. Compare the eager and compiled layer at batch 1, sequence 1. Where does eager execution spend its time?

Section 4.4 looks inside a run: which operations take the time, using MAX’s profiling tools.

4.4 Profiling

A benchmark tells you how long something takes. A profile tells you where the time goes, which is what you need before optimizing anything. This section profiles at three levels: a whole generation run, the kernels a compiled graph launches, and the parts of your own model.

Show the setup 21 lines

import warnings

import plotly.graph_objects as go

from minillm import viz
from minillm.bench import benchmark
from minillm.device import pick_device

from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU
config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()

Profile a whole run

max generate takes a --profile flag. On an NVIDIA GPU with Nsight Systems installed, it records a kernel trace and prints the kernels that took the most GPU time. It always profiles the Python side with cProfile too, which answers the first question to ask of any run: is the time in your model, or in the code around it?

!max generate --model HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct \
    --quantization-encoding float32 --prompt "hello" --max-new-tokens 16 \
    --num-warmups 1 --profile 2>&1 | grep -A 8 "=== Top"

=== Top 15 Python/CPU functions (94.4% of CPU time, 307.64 ms total) ===
     %       total     calls  function
 49.0%   150.85 ms        64  ~:0<built-in method numpy.from_dlpack>
 43.0%   132.25 ms        32  max/engine/api.py:118(_Model_execute)
  0.3%     1.04 ms        16  max/pipelines/lib/pipeline_variants/text_generation.py:597(execute)
  0.3%   970.84 us        16  max/pipelines/sampling/sampling_logits_processor.py:109(_packed_rows_to_device)
  0.3%   781.75 us        16  max/pipelines/lib/module_registration.py:763(prepare_initial_token_inputs)
  0.2%   589.67 us        16  max/pipelines/sampling/sampling_logits_processor.py:695(create)
  0.2%   586.50 us        16  max/pipelines/lib/pipeline_variants/text_generation.py:610(_execute)

The time is in the model: _Model_execute, the call into the compiled graph, and, on a GPU, numpy.from_dlpack, which reads the logits back to the host and so waits for the GPU to finish its work. That wait is model time too, a common trap when reading profiles of asynchronous devices. On this page’s machine, an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU, the two take over 90% of the profiled time. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. The pipeline around the model (tokenization, scheduling, sampling, and the KV cache bookkeeping) takes under a percent per function. For this model, any speedup has to come from the compiled graph itself.

See which kernels run

MODULAR_DEBUG=op-log-level=TRACE makes the runtime log every operation it launches and completes. Trace the MLP from Section 1.4. The setting must be in place when the process starts, so the code runs as a separate script:

%%writefile trace_mlp.py
import numpy as np
from max.dtype import DType
from max.graph import TensorType
from max.tensor import Tensor, default_device, default_dtype

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.layers import MLP

DEVICE = pick_device()
with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    mlp = MLP(LlamaConfig())
run = mlp.compile(TensorType(DType.float32, ("seq_len", 576), device=DEVICE))
print("--- run ---", flush=True)
run(Tensor(np.ones((4, 576), np.float32), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()

Overwriting trace_mlp.py

!MODULAR_DEBUG=op-log-level=TRACE python trace_mlp.py 2>&1 | sed -n '/--- run ---/,$p' | grep LAUNCH | grep -v 'LAUNCH matmul[^ ]'

[OP] LAUNCH matmul [id=22] target=gpu:0
[OP] LAUNCH Epilogue(matmul, Elementwise(silu, mul), operandIndices=[0]) [id=24] target=gpu:0
[OP] LAUNCH matmul [id=26] target=gpu:0

Each matmul logs a second LAUNCH line from inside the kernel, whose name MAX joins to the graph operation’s name with no space (matmulmo.matmul); the grep -v drops those, which leaves one line per kernel the graph launches.

The MLP has five operations in your Python (three matmuls, silu, and a multiply), more in the traced graph you saw in Section 3.2, and three kernels at run time. The Epilogue entry is the second matmul with silu and mul fused into it: the activation is applied as each tile of the matmul’s output is produced, before it is written to memory. That fusion is why the hand-written SwiGLU kernel in Section 3.1 had little left to fuse, and the trace is how you’d find out. The handbook’s kernel optimization for LLM inference page explains fusion and why it matters most on GPUs.

Profile by decomposition

To find where time goes inside your own model, time its parts separately with the harness from Section 4.3. Compile one layer’s attention, MLP, and norm, and the LM head, and time each at two shapes: one decode step (1 token) and a prefill (256 tokens). Each time is multiplied by how often the part runs in the model: 30 attention and MLP blocks, and 61 norms (two per layer plus the final one).

Show the code 31 lines 
model = load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE)
hidden = TensorType(
    DType.float32, ("batch", "seq_len", config.hidden_size), device=DEVICE
)
layer = model.model.layers[0]
parts = {
    "attention (x30)": (layer.self_attn.compile(hidden), 30),
    "MLP (x30)": (layer.mlp.compile(hidden), 30),
    "norms (x61)": (layer.input_layernorm.compile(hidden), 61),
    "LM head": (model.lm_head.compile(hidden), 1),
}

shares, all_times = {}, {}
for tokens in [1, 256]:
    x = Tensor(
        np.random.default_rng(0)
        .standard_normal((1, tokens, config.hidden_size))
        .astype(np.float32),
        device=DEVICE,
    )
    times = {
        name: benchmark(lambda: run(x).to_numpy(), iterations=30).median_ms
        * count
        for name, (run, count) in parts.items()
    }
    total = sum(times.values())
    shares[tokens] = {name: t / total for name, t in times.items()}
    all_times[tokens] = times
    print(f"{tokens} token(s): {total:.1f} ms in total")
    for name, t in times.items():
        print(f"  {name:16} {t:7.2f} ms  {t / total:5.0%}")

1 token(s): 49.2 ms in total
  attention (x30)    23.31 ms    47%
  MLP (x30)          10.01 ms    20%
  norms (x61)        13.80 ms    28%
  LM head             2.05 ms     4%
256 token(s): 156.0 ms in total
  attention (x30)    73.32 ms    47%
  MLP (x30)          33.32 ms    21%
  norms (x61)        20.77 ms    13%
  LM head            28.61 ms    18%

    [
        go.Bar(
            y=rows,

            name=name,
            orientation="h",
            marker=dict(color=color, line=dict(color=viz.SURFACE, width=2)),
            customdata=[all_times[1][name], all_times[256][name]],
            hovertemplate=name

        )
        for name, color in zip(parts, viz.SERIES)
    ]
)

    title="Where the model's time goes",
    barmode="stack",
    height=300,
    legend_traceorder="normal",

    yaxis=dict(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False),

Compare the two bars. The shares below were measured on this page’s Metal GPU.

Attention takes the largest share at both shapes, about 40%. Its cost is the only one that grows with the square of the sequence length, so its share grows on longer prompts; at 256 tokens the prompt is still short enough that it barely moves.

The LM head’s share grows the most, from a few percent to over a tenth. It holds more weights than all 30 layers’ attention put together (49,152 × 576), and here it runs on every one of the 256 prompt positions. The cached model from Section 1.8 computes it for the last position only, which keeps it small during prefill.

The norms look expensive at decode, over a quarter of the time, but that is mostly an artifact of this method. Each separately compiled call pays a fixed cost to launch its work and read back the result, and the decomposition counts that cost 61 times for the norms; the whole compiled model runs as one graph and pays it once. Time the whole model as one compiled graph to see how large that overhead is:

whole = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)
for tokens in [1, 256]:
    ids = Tensor(np.zeros((1, tokens), np.int64), device=DEVICE)
    whole_ms = benchmark(lambda: whole(ids).to_numpy(), iterations=30).median_ms
    parts_ms = sum(all_times[tokens].values())
    print(
        f"{tokens:3} token(s): whole model {whole_ms:6.1f} ms,"
        f" sum of parts {parts_ms:6.1f} ms ({parts_ms / whole_ms:.1f}x)"
    )

  1 token(s): whole model   24.0 ms, sum of parts   49.2 ms (2.0x)
256 token(s): whole model   84.0 ms, sum of parts  156.0 ms (1.9x)

At both shapes the whole model takes less than half the time its parts add up to. The difference is what the 122 separate calls cost: each one launches its work and reads its result back to the host, and the whole graph does both once. So a decomposition ranks the parts, but its shares overstate small operations such as the norms. Check a surprising number with the kernel trace before acting on it.

Every optimization starts here: find the largest share, decide whether it is memory-bound or compute-bound (Section 4.3), then look at its kernels (the trace above).

With Nsight Systems

On NVIDIA GPUs, Nsight Systems answers the same questions: set MODULAR_ENABLE_PROFILING=detailed and run your program under nsys profile, or pass --profile to max generate. max benchmark --trace does the same for a server that already runs under nsys launch, started with --gpu-profiling detailed. MAX marks its operations as named ranges in the timeline, so you can match GPU kernels to the operations that launched them. MAX’s guide to GPU profiling with Nsight Systems covers the setup. The handbook’s kernel optimization tools page surveys the tools.

5. A serving engine

The model from Chapter 1 generates for one prompt at a time. A serving engine takes requests from many users at once and keeps them all moving. This chapter builds the core of such an engine, one technique per section.

5.2 Continuous batchingA scheduler, and three batching policies compared on the same workload.

5.4 Prefix cachingSharing the cache blocks of a common prompt prefix between requests.

Related handbook pages: batching, PagedAttention, prefix caching, and speculative decoding.

5.1 The life of a request

So far your model served one prompt at a time, from a notebook. A serving engine takes requests from many users at once and keeps them all moving: it admits new requests, runs the model, hands each request its next token, and stops each one when it is done. This chapter builds that engine over five sections, on top of a new model interface designed for it.

MAX Serve and other inference engines share this structure:

The handbook’s How does LLM inference work? page describes the same loop.

Show the setup 24 lines

Show the setup 22 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

import math
from collections.abc import Callable

from max import functional as F
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn import ModuleList
from max.nn.kernels import flash_attention
from max.tensor import Tensor

from minillm.attention import Attention
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import rotate_half
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer, LlamaForCausalLM

KVPair = tuple[Tensor, Tensor]
Step = Callable[..., tuple[Tensor, list[KVPair]]]
# Masked scores use a large finite value instead of -inf: attention kernels
# process keys in blocks, and a row whose first block is fully -inf computes
# exp(-inf - -inf) = NaN, which then spreads through the values to other rows.
MASKED = -1e30

Saved to minillm/step.py.

A model step for an engine

The cached model from Section 1.8 kept its cache inside and handled one sequence. An engine needs one call that advances several sequences, each with its own history, by any number of tokens. The step model does that. Each call takes, for every sequence in the batch:

  • the new tokens and their positions,
  • an attention mask saying which past and new positions each token may see,
  • the past keys and values,

and returns the next-token logits plus only the new keys and values. The model keeps no state. Where the cache lives and how it is laid out is up to the engine, and Section 5.3 and Section 5.4 change exactly that.

Positions now differ per sequence, so RoPE takes a position for each token instead of a start offset:

def rope_at(
    x: Tensor, positions: Tensor, head_dim: int, theta: float
) -> Tensor:
    """Rotates ``x``, ``[batch, q, heads, head_dim]``, for per-token
    ``positions``, ``[batch, q]``."""
    inv_freq = Tensor(
        [theta ** (-i / head_dim) for i in range(0, head_dim, 2)],
        dtype=DType.float32,
        device=x.mesh,
    )
    batch, q = positions.shape
    angles = positions.reshape([batch, q, 1]) * inv_freq
    angles = F.concat([angles, angles], axis=-1).reshape(
        [batch, q, 1, head_dim]
    )
    return x * F.cos(angles) + rotate_half(x) * F.sin(angles)

Saved to minillm/step.py.

The attention layer projects only the new tokens, appends their keys and values to the past ones, and attends over both. The mask has one column per past and new position:

Show StepAttention 27 lines 
class StepAttention(Attention):
    """Attends from new tokens to the past keys and values plus their own."""

    def forward(
        self, x: Tensor, positions: Tensor, mask: Tensor, past: KVPair
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, KVPair]:
        batch, q, _ = x.shape
        query = self.q_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, q, self.num_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        key = self.k_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, q, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        value = self.v_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, q, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        query = rope_at(query, positions, self.head_dim, self.rope_theta)
        key = rope_at(key, positions, self.head_dim, self.rope_theta)
        all_keys = F.concat([past[0], key], axis=1)
        all_values = F.concat([past[1], value], axis=1)
        # The mask has one column per past and new position.
        mask = F.rebind(mask, [batch, q, all_keys.shape[1]])
        out = flash_attention(
            query, all_keys, all_values, 1 / math.sqrt(self.head_dim), mask
        ).rebind_mapping(query.mapping)
        out = out.reshape([batch, q, self.num_heads * self.head_dim])
        return self.o_proj(out), (key, value)

Saved to minillm/step.py.

The decoder layer and the model pass the new arguments through, and the model collects each layer’s new keys and values:

Show StepLayer, StepModel 38 lines 
class StepLayer(DecoderLayer):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.self_attn = StepAttention(config)

    def forward(
        self, x: Tensor, positions: Tensor, mask: Tensor, past: KVPair
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, KVPair]:
        attn, kv = self.self_attn(
            self.input_layernorm(x), positions, mask, past
        )
        x = x + attn
        return x + self.mlp(self.post_attention_layernorm(x)), kv


class StepModel(LlamaForCausalLM):
    """Advances a batch of sequences: returns each one's next-token logits
    and the new tokens' keys and values for every layer."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.model.layers = ModuleList(
            [StepLayer(config) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)]
        )

    def forward(
        self,
        tokens: Tensor,
        positions: Tensor,
        mask: Tensor,
        past: list[KVPair],
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, list[KVPair]]:
        x = self.model.embed_tokens(tokens)
        new_kv = []
        for layer, layer_past in zip(self.model.layers, past, strict=True):
            x, kv = layer(x, positions, mask, layer_past)
            new_kv.append(kv)
        return self.lm_head(self.model.norm(x)[:, -1]), new_kv

Saved to minillm/step.py.

Compile it once. Every dimension that changes between calls is symbolic: the batch size, the number of new tokens q, and the past length:

import numpy as np
from max.driver import Device
from max.graph import TensorType


def compile_step(model: StepModel, config: LlamaConfig, device: Device) -> Step:
    """Compiles ``model`` for any batch size, number of new tokens, and past
    length."""
    kv = TensorType(
        DType.float32,
        ("batch", "past", config.num_key_value_heads, config.head_dim),
        device=device,
    )
    return model.compile(
        TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "q"), device=device),
        TensorType(DType.float32, ("batch", "q"), device=device),
        TensorType(DType.float32, ("batch", "q", "kv"), device=device),
        [(kv, kv)] * config.num_hidden_layers,
    )

Saved to minillm/step.py.

start = time.perf_counter()
weights = load_checkpoint()
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, weights, DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)
print(f"compiled the step model in {time.perf_counter() - start:.0f} s")

compiled the step model in 13 s

The cache and the batch

The engine stores each sequence’s keys and values in a cache object. The first version is the simplest: one growing array per layer. Every later cache has the same interface (length, num_layers, read, and append), so run_step and the step model work with all of them unchanged.

class ContiguousCache:
    """One sequence's keys and values: a growing array per layer."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        shape = (0, config.num_key_value_heads, config.head_dim)
        self.layers = [
            [np.zeros(shape, np.float32), np.zeros(shape, np.float32)]
            for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
        ]

    @property
    def length(self) -> int:
        return len(self.layers[0][0])

    @property
    def num_layers(self) -> int:
        return len(self.layers)

    def read(self, layer: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
        return self.layers[layer][0], self.layers[layer][1]

    def append(self, layer: int, keys: np.ndarray, values: np.ndarray) -> None:
        entry = self.layers[layer]
        entry[0] = np.concatenate([entry[0], keys])
        entry[1] = np.concatenate([entry[1], values])

Saved to minillm/step.py.

To run several sequences in one step, the engine packs them into rectangular arrays. Sequences add different numbers of new tokens, so shorter ones are left-padded, and the mask is built per token: a new token may see its own sequence’s past and the new tokens up to itself, and nothing else. A padding token sees only itself, so its row of the softmax stays well defined; its output is discarded. Masked positions get a large negative number instead of \(-\infty\). If a padding row’s first block of keys were entirely \(-\infty\), a blockwise attention kernel would compute \(e^{-\infty + \infty}\), which is NaN. In the next layer that NaN would become the padding position’s keys and values, and the real tokens of the same sequence would pick it up even though the mask hides that position.

Show run_step 45 lines 
def run_step(
    step: Step, caches: list, new_tokens: list[list[int]], device: Device
) -> np.ndarray:
    """Advances each sequence by its ``new_tokens``, appends their keys and
    values to its cache, and returns next-token logits, ``[batch, vocab]``."""
    batch, q = len(caches), max(len(t) for t in new_tokens)
    past = max(c.length for c in caches)
    tokens = np.zeros((batch, q), np.int64)
    positions = np.zeros((batch, q), np.float32)
    mask = np.full((batch, q, past + q), MASKED, np.float32)
    for i, (cache, new) in enumerate(zip(caches, new_tokens, strict=True)):
        pad, n = q - len(new), cache.length
        tokens[i, pad:] = new
        positions[i, pad:] = np.arange(n, n + len(new))
        mask[i, pad:, :n] = 0.0  # every new token sees the sequence's past
        mask[i, pad:, past + pad :] = np.triu(
            np.full((len(new), len(new)), MASKED), 1
        )
        mask[i, np.arange(pad), past + np.arange(pad)] = (
            0.0  # padding sees itself
        )
    past_kv = []
    for layer in range(caches[0].num_layers):
        keys = np.zeros(
            (batch, past, *caches[0].read(layer)[0].shape[1:]), np.float32
        )
        values = np.zeros_like(keys)
        for i, cache in enumerate(caches):
            k, v = cache.read(layer)
            keys[i, : len(k)], values[i, : len(v)] = k, v
        past_kv.append(
            (Tensor(keys, device=device), Tensor(values, device=device))
        )
    logits, new_kv = step(
        Tensor(tokens, device=device),
        Tensor(positions, device=device),
        Tensor(mask, device=device),
        past_kv,
    )
    for layer, (k, v) in enumerate(new_kv):
        k, v = k.to_numpy(), v.to_numpy()
        for i, (cache, new) in enumerate(zip(caches, new_tokens, strict=True)):
            pad = q - len(new)
            cache.append(layer, k[i, pad:], v[i, pad:])
    return logits.to_numpy()

Saved to minillm/step.py.

Check the step against the model from Section 1.6 by generating greedily for two prompts of different lengths in the same steps:

Show greedy_reference 12 lines 
full = load_model(config, weights, DEVICE).compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq"), device=DEVICE)
)


def greedy_reference(ids: list[int], n: int) -> list[int]:
    """Generates ``n`` tokens greedily with the full model, no cache."""
    ids = list(ids)
    for _ in range(n):
        logits = full(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()
        ids.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
    return ids[-n:]

prompts = [
    tokenizer([t])["input_ids"][0]
    for t in ["The capital of France is", "Once upon a time, there was a"]
]
caches = [ContiguousCache(config) for _ in prompts]
outputs = [[int(t)] for t in run_step(step, caches, prompts, DEVICE).argmax(-1)]
for _ in range(11):
    next_ids = run_step(
        step, caches, [[o[-1]] for o in outputs], DEVICE
    ).argmax(-1)
    for out, t in zip(outputs, next_ids):
        out.append(int(t))
for prompt, out in zip(prompts, outputs):
    print(out == greedy_reference(prompt, 12), repr(tokenizer.decode(out)))
    assert out == greedy_reference(prompt, 12)

True ' Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital'
True ' young woman named Sarah who was struggling to find her place in'

Both sequences match the reference, even though they have different lengths and shared every step.

    recorded.update(
        tokens=tokens.to_numpy(),
        positions=positions.to_numpy(),
        mask=mask.to_numpy(),
    )
    return step(tokens, positions, mask, past)

    step, caches[:1], [prompts[1][:6]], DEVICE

Sequence 0’s single new token sits in the last row, after three padding rows that each see only themselves. It sees its 6 cached positions and itself. Sequence 1 has no cached tokens, so its 6 cached positions are masked in every row. They hold zeros, there only because the batch’s longest past is 6. Its 4 new tokens form the triangle of the causal mask.

A first engine: one request at a time

A request carries its prompt, how many tokens it may generate, and timestamps for the latency metrics from Section 1.1, such as time to first token (TTFT). Every engine in this chapter serves the same workload: eight requests that arrive at once, each asking for between 8 and 32 new tokens:

from dataclasses import dataclass, field


@dataclass
class Request:
    prompt: list[int]
    max_new_tokens: int
    output: list[int] = field(default_factory=list)
    arrived: float = 0.0
    first_token: float | None = None
    finished: float | None = None

    @property
    def done(self) -> bool:
        return len(self.output) >= self.max_new_tokens

Saved to minillm/step.py.

import time
from collections.abc import Mapping

Tokenizer = Callable[[list[str]], Mapping[str, list[list[int]]]]

WORKLOAD = [
    ("Tell me about Paris.", 32),
    ("Write a haiku about the sea.", 24),
    ("What is 2 + 2?", 8),
    ("List three colors.", 16),
    ("Explain gravity in one sentence.", 32),
    ("Name a fruit.", 8),
    ("Describe a cat.", 24),
    ("Say hello in French.", 16),
]


def make_workload(tokenizer: Tokenizer) -> list[Request]:
    """Returns the chapter's eight requests, all arriving now."""
    now = time.perf_counter()
    return [
        Request(tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0], n, arrived=now)
        for text, n in WORKLOAD
    ]

Saved to minillm/step.py.

The simplest engine serves requests in arrival order, each to completion, one at a time:

Show report 9 lines 
def report(requests: list[Request]) -> None:
    ttft = [(r.first_token - r.arrived) * 1000 for r in requests]
    total = max(r.finished for r in requests) - min(r.arrived for r in requests)
    tokens = sum(len(r.output) for r in requests)
    print(
        f"TTFT: first request {ttft[0]:.0f} ms, "
        f"median {np.median(ttft):.0f} ms, worst {max(ttft):.0f} ms"
    )
    print(f"{tokens} tokens in {total:.1f} s = {tokens / total:.1f} tokens/s")

def serve_one_at_a_time(requests: list[Request]) -> None:
    for request in requests:
        cache = ContiguousCache(config)
        logits = run_step(step, [cache], [request.prompt], DEVICE)
        while True:
            request.output.append(int(logits[0].argmax()))
            request.first_token = request.first_token or time.perf_counter()
            if request.done:
                break
            logits = run_step(step, [cache], [[request.output[-1]]], DEVICE)
        request.finished = time.perf_counter()


sequential = make_workload(tokenizer)
serve_one_at_a_time(sequential)
report(sequential)

TTFT: first request 296 ms, median 16553 ms, worst 30666 ms
160 tokens in 35.6 s = 4.5 tokens/s

Eight users sent their requests at the same moment. The first one got a token almost at once. The last one waited for every request ahead of it to finish, so its time to first token is most of the total run time. Section 4.3 measured a decoder layer’s decode step for 32 sequences at about 1.2 to 1.3 times the cost of a step for one, on the Mac the book was built on, so this engine leaves most of the hardware’s capacity unused.

The engines in this chapter favor clarity over speed. On every step, run_step packs each layer’s past keys and values into NumPy arrays, copies them to the device, and copies the new ones back, so a step costs more here than the cached decode step in Section 1.8. Compare this chapter’s timings with each other, not with Chapter 1’s. Section 5.2 runs several requests in each step.

The handbook’s LLM inference metrics page explains why serving systems track TTFT percentiles as well as averages.

Exercises

  1. Serve the workload in order of max_new_tokens, shortest first. What happens to the median and worst TTFT? What would a user with a long request think of this policy?
  2. Run run_step with three prompts in one call and check each against greedy_reference.
  3. Print the mask run_step builds for two sequences with 2 and 4 new tokens and past lengths 3 and 1. Check each row by hand.

Next

Section 5.2 replaces the one-at-a-time loop with continuous batching.

5.2 Continuous batching

The engine from Section 5.1 ran one sequence per step, while Section 4.3 showed that a decode step for many sequences costs little more than a step for one. Batching uses that spare capacity, and the way you batch decides who waits. This section adds a scheduler to the engine and compares three policies on the same workload.

Show the setup 31 lines

    load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
