The engine

The engine holds two lists: requests waiting to start and requests running. Each call to step does one model step:

  • If there is room in the batch and requests are waiting, it admits them and runs their prefill: all their prompt tokens, in one batched step.
  • Otherwise it runs a decode step: one new token for every running request, in one batched step.

Finished requests leave the batch after every step. That is continuous batching: the batch is re-formed at every step, so a waiting request starts as soon as a slot frees up. Setting continuous=False gives static batching instead: a batch is admitted together and must finish completely before the next one starts.

Running prefill and decode in separate steps is a simple policy that gives new requests priority, at the cost of pausing the running requests’ decode for a step. Production engines can also split a long prompt into chunks (chunked prefill) and run prefill chunks and decodes in the same step, so one big prefill doesn’t stall everyone. MAX Serve chunks long prefills by default (--enable-chunked-prefill) and batches them with decodes when you pass --enable-in-flight-batching.

Show Engine 86 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

import time
from collections import deque
from collections.abc import Callable

from max.driver import Device

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.step import ContiguousCache, Request, Step, run_step


class Engine:
    """Serves requests with a batching scheduler and greedy decoding."""

    def __init__(
        self,
        step: Step,
        config: LlamaConfig,
        device: Device,
        max_batch: int = 4,
        continuous: bool = True,
        new_cache: Callable[[LlamaConfig], object] = ContiguousCache,
    ) -> None:
        self.step_fn = step
        self.config = config
        self.device = device
        self.max_batch = max_batch
        self.continuous = continuous
        self.new_cache = new_cache
        self.waiting: deque[Request] = deque()
        self.running: list[tuple[Request, object]] = []
        self.steps = 0

    def add(self, request: Request) -> None:
        self.waiting.append(request)

    def has_work(self) -> bool:
        return bool(self.waiting or self.running)

    def step(self) -> None:
        room = self.max_batch - len(self.running)
        can_admit = (
            self.waiting and room > 0 and (self.continuous or not self.running)
        )
        if can_admit:
            admitted = [
                self.waiting.popleft()
                for _ in range(min(room, len(self.waiting)))
            ]
            batch = [(r, self.new_cache(self.config)) for r in admitted]
            inputs = [self.prefill_tokens(r, c) for r, c in batch]
        else:
            batch = self.running
            inputs = [[r.output[-1]] for r, _ in batch]
        logits = run_step(
            self.step_fn, [c for _, c in batch], inputs, self.device
        )
        self.steps += 1
        now = time.perf_counter()
        for (request, _), token in zip(batch, logits.argmax(-1), strict=True):
            request.output.append(int(token))
            if request.first_token is None:
                request.first_token = now
            if request.done:
                request.finished = now
        if can_admit:
            for request, cache in batch:
                self.prefilled(request, cache)
            self.running += batch
        self.running = [(r, c) for r, c in self.running if not r.done]
        self.release_finished(batch)

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: object) -> list[int]:
        """Returns the prompt tokens to compute; Section 5.4 skips cached ones."""
        return request.prompt

    def prefilled(self, request: Request, cache: object) -> None:
        """Runs after a request's prefill; Section 5.4 uses it."""

    def release_finished(self, batch: list) -> None:
        """Frees the caches of finished requests; Section 5.3 needs this."""

    def run(self) -> None:
        while self.has_work():
            self.step()

Saved to minillm/engine.py.

A workload

Serve the eight requests from Section 5.1 under three policies. A recording subclass of Engine notes which request holds each batch slot at every step, for the figures below:

Show RecordingEngine, run_policy, summarize 51 lines 
class RecordingEngine(Engine):
    """Records, per step, the request in each batch slot and what it did."""

    def step(self) -> None:
        before = {id(r): len(r.output) for r in self.requests}
        super().step()
        ran = [r for r in self.requests if len(r.output) > before[id(r)]]
        for r in ran:
            if r not in self.slots:
                self.slots[self.slots.index(None)] = r
        row = []
        for r in self.slots:
            if r is None:
                row.append(None)
            elif r not in ran:
                row.append((self.requests.index(r), "waits"))
            elif before[id(r)] == 0:
                row.append((self.requests.index(r), "prefill"))
            else:
                row.append((self.requests.index(r), "decode"))
        self.history.append(row)
        self.slots = [None if r is None or r.done else r for r in self.slots]


def run_policy(max_batch: int, continuous: bool) -> tuple[list, Engine]:
    engine = RecordingEngine(
        step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=max_batch, continuous=continuous
    )
    engine.requests = make_workload(tokenizer)
    engine.slots = [None] * max_batch
    engine.history = []
    for r in engine.requests:
        engine.add(r)
    engine.run()
    return engine.requests, engine


def summarize(name: str, requests: list[Request], engine: Engine) -> dict:
    ttft = [(r.first_token - r.arrived) * 1000 for r in requests]
    total = max(r.finished for r in requests) - min(r.arrived for r in requests)
    tokens = sum(len(r.output) for r in requests)
    print(
        f"{name:22} {engine.steps:4} steps  {tokens / total:5.1f} tokens/s  "
        f"TTFT median {np.median(ttft):6.0f} ms  worst {max(ttft):6.0f} ms"
    )
    return {
        "ttft": ttft,
        "outputs": [r.output for r in requests],
        "requests": requests,
        "history": engine.history,
    }

results = {}
for name, max_batch, continuous in [
    ("one at a time", 1, True),
    ("static batches of 4", 4, False),
    ("continuous, batch 4", 4, True),
]:
    results[name] = summarize(name, *run_policy(max_batch, continuous))

static batches of 4      64 steps    3.2 tokens/s  TTFT median   6450 ms  worst  12693 ms
continuous, batch 4      50 steps    9.3 tokens/s  TTFT median   2151 ms  worst   8903 ms

Batching changes only the order of the work, so greedy decoding must produce the same tokens for every request under every policy:

Slide between the policies. One at a time, each request waits for every request before it. Static batches run in two rounds, and the second round can’t start until the slowest request of the first finishes. Continuous batching starts a waiting request as soon as any slot frees up.

Both batching policies cut the number of model steps, from 160 to 64 for static batches and 50 for continuous batching, so they finish the workload sooner and raise throughput. They differ in who waits:

  • Static batching admits four requests and runs until the longest of them finishes. The second group waits for that, even after the short requests in the first group are done and their slots sit empty: the grey holes in the slot chart.
  • Continuous batching refills a slot as soon as a request finishes, so waiting requests start sooner, and the batch stays full while any request is waiting.

Production engines, MAX Serve among them, use continuous batching with much larger batches, bounded by the memory the KV cache needs. That bound is the subject of Section 5.3. The handbook’s static, dynamic, and continuous batching page compares the policies in production terms.

Exercises

  1. Run continuous batching with max_batch 2, 4, and 8. How do throughput and worst-case TTFT change?
  2. Make requests arrive over time: add each one after a few engine steps instead of all at the start. Which policy handles bursts better?
  3. Implement a mixed step: admit new requests’ prefills in the same step as running requests’ decodes. What does it cost the running requests?

Next

Section 5.3 replaces the contiguous cache with a paged one, so the batch can grow without wasting memory.

5.3 A paged KV cache

With continuous batching, memory limits how many requests can be in flight: every request needs room for its keys and values, and Section 1.8 measured 45 KiB per token for SmolLM2-135M in float32. The question is how to hand out that memory. A server doesn’t know how long a reply will be when it starts, so the simplest scheme reserves room for the maximum length, and most of that room goes unused. (ContiguousCache avoids the reservation by copying its arrays on every append, which costs time instead.) This section replaces the contiguous cache with a paged one, the idea behind vLLM’s PagedAttention and MAX Serve’s paged KV cache.

Show the setup 24 lines 
import logging
import warnings

import plotly.graph_objects as go

from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.step import Request, StepModel, compile_step, make_workload
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)

Blocks and block tables

Paging works like virtual memory. The cache memory is one pool of fixed-size blocks, each holding the keys and values of block_size tokens. A sequence owns a list of block numbers, its block table, and takes a new block from the pool only when its last block fills. A sequence’s blocks needn’t be next to each other, so free memory never breaks into gaps too small to use; the only waste is the unfilled part of each sequence’s last block. A finished sequence returns its blocks to the pool at once.

Show BlockPool, PagedCache 79 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

import numpy as np

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig


class BlockPool:
    """Fixed-size blocks of KV cache memory, shared by all sequences."""

    def __init__(
        self, config: LlamaConfig, num_blocks: int, block_size: int = 16
    ) -> None:
        self.block_size = block_size
        shape = (
            num_blocks,
            block_size,
            config.num_key_value_heads,
            config.head_dim,
        )
        self.keys = [
            np.zeros(shape, np.float32) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
        ]
        self.values = [
            np.zeros(shape, np.float32) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
        ]
        self.free = list(range(num_blocks))

    @property
    def num_blocks(self) -> int:
        return len(self.keys[0])

    def allocate(self) -> int:
        if not self.free:
            raise MemoryError("the KV cache pool is full")
        return self.free.pop()

    def available(self) -> int:
        """Returns how many blocks ``allocate`` can still hand out."""
        return len(self.free)

    def release(self, blocks: list[int]) -> None:
        self.free.extend(blocks)


class PagedCache:
    """One sequence's keys and values, stored in pool blocks."""

    def __init__(self, pool: BlockPool) -> None:
        self.pool = pool
        self.blocks: list[int] = []
        self.length = 0

    @property
    def num_layers(self) -> int:
        return len(self.pool.keys)

    def read(self, layer: int) -> tuple[np.ndarray, np.ndarray]:
        keys = self.pool.keys[layer][self.blocks].reshape(
            -1, *self.pool.keys[layer].shape[2:]
        )
        values = self.pool.values[layer][self.blocks].reshape(keys.shape)
        return keys[: self.length], values[: self.length]

    def append(self, layer: int, keys: np.ndarray, values: np.ndarray) -> None:
        size, start = self.pool.block_size, self.length
        if layer == 0:  # every layer stores the same positions
            while len(self.blocks) * size < start + len(keys):
                self.blocks.append(self.pool.allocate())
        for i in range(len(keys)):
            block, slot = self.blocks[(start + i) // size], (start + i) % size
            self.pool.keys[layer][block, slot] = keys[i]
            self.pool.values[layer][block, slot] = values[i]
        if layer == self.num_layers - 1:
            self.length += len(keys)

    def release(self) -> None:
        self.pool.release(self.blocks)
        self.blocks = []

Saved to minillm/paged.py.

read gathers a sequence’s blocks into one array for the step model. That copy keeps the code simple. Production engines skip it: their attention kernel reads the blocks in place through the block table, which takes a custom kernel like the ones Section 3.1 shows how to write. The engine needs three changes. It admits waiting requests in arrival order while the pool has blocks for their prompts. When a decode step needs new blocks and the pool is empty, it preempts the newest running request: it frees that request’s blocks and puts it back at the front of the queue, to recompute its prompt and the tokens it has generated so far when it runs again. And it returns a finished request’s blocks to the pool.

Show PagedEngine 68 lines 
from minillm.engine import Engine
from minillm.step import Request


class PagedEngine(Engine):
    """An engine whose requests share one pool of KV cache blocks."""

    def __init__(self, *args, pool: BlockPool, **kwargs) -> None:
        super().__init__(*args, new_cache=lambda _: PagedCache(pool), **kwargs)
        self.pool = pool
        self.preemptions = 0

    def step(self) -> None:
        # Admit waiting requests in arrival order while their tokens so far fit.
        spare, admitted = self.pool.available(), 0
        for request in self.waiting:
            if self.blocks_needed(request) > spare:
                break
            spare -= self.blocks_needed(request)
            admitted += 1
        held = [self.waiting.pop() for _ in range(len(self.waiting) - admitted)]
        held.reverse()  # pop() took them from the back
        prefill = (
            self.waiting
            and len(self.running) < self.max_batch
            and (self.continuous or not self.running)
        )
        if not prefill and not self.running and held:
            raise MemoryError(
                "a waiting request needs more blocks than the pool has"
            )
        preempted = [] if prefill else self.preempt_until_decode_fits()
        super().step()
        # Preempted requests go to the front of the queue, as in MAX.
        self.waiting.extend(held)
        self.waiting.extendleft(reversed(preempted))

    def preempt_until_decode_fits(self) -> list[Request]:
        """Frees the newest requests' blocks until every running request can
        store its next token, and returns them to recompute later."""
        preempted = []
        while self.blocks_to_grow() > self.pool.available():
            if len(self.running) == 1:
                raise MemoryError(
                    "a request needs more blocks than the pool has"
                )
            request, cache = self.running.pop()
            cache.release()
            preempted.insert(0, request)
            self.preemptions += 1
        return preempted

    def blocks_to_grow(self) -> int:
        size = self.pool.block_size
        return sum(c.length + 1 > len(c.blocks) * size for _, c in self.running)

    def blocks_needed(self, request: Request) -> int:
        # A preempted request recomputes the tokens it already generated.
        tokens = len(request.prompt) + len(request.output)
        return -(-tokens // self.pool.block_size)

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: PagedCache) -> list[int]:
        return request.prompt + request.output

    def release_finished(self, batch: list) -> None:
        for request, cache in batch:
            if request.done:
                cache.release()

Saved to minillm/paged.py.

The tokens match, and every block is back in the pool after the run. To watch the block tables, run the workload again on a smaller pool of 24 blocks of 8 tokens, and record every running request’s block table after each engine step:

class RecordingEngine(PagedEngine):
    def step(self) -> None:
        super().step()
        self.snapshots.append(
            [
                (requests.index(r), list(c.blocks), c.length)
                for r, c in self.running
            ]
        )


requests = make_workload(tokenizer)
recorder = RecordingEngine(
    step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=BlockPool(config, 24, 8)
)
recorder.snapshots = []
for r in requests:
    recorder.add(r)
recorder.run()
print("preemptions:", recorder.preemptions)

preemptions: 0

On the left, each running request’s block table lists its logical blocks in order, with the fill of its last block. On the right is the pool, and each line links a logical block to the physical block that holds it. Slide through the steps:

Blocks are handed out from wherever they are free, so a request’s blocks end up scattered across the pool, and a finished request’s blocks go straight to the next request. Only the block table records their order. This pool was big enough that no request was preempted; exercise 1 shrinks it until some are.

Why it matters: memory

Compare the memory each scheme ties up for a batch of four of these requests. A contiguous cache can’t grow in place, so when a request starts it must reserve room for the longest the request could get. That is SmolLM2’s full 8192-token context, or at best the request’s max_tokens limit, which API clients often set well above the length of the replies they get; the table below assumes 512. Paging allocates only what each sequence holds, rounded up to whole blocks of \(B\) tokens:

\[\text{blocks for } n \text{ tokens} = \left\lceil \frac{n}{B} \right\rceil\]

per_token = (
    2
    * config.num_hidden_layers
    * config.num_key_value_heads
    * config.head_dim
    * 4
)
batch = 4
actual = [len(r.prompt) + len(r.output) for r in paged_requests]
reserve_max_context = batch * config.max_position_embeddings
reserve_max_tokens = batch * (max(len(r.prompt) for r in paged_requests) + 512)
paged_tokens = sum(sorted(-(-n // 16) * 16 for n in actual)[-batch:])

rows = [
    (name, f"{tokens:,}", f"{tokens * per_token / 2**20:.1f} MiB")
    for name, tokens in [
        ("reserve full context per request", reserve_max_context),
        ("reserve max_tokens=512 per request", reserve_max_tokens),
        ("paged, 16-token blocks (4 largest)", paged_tokens),
    ]
]
header = ("scheme", "tokens reserved", "memory")
widths = [max(len(row[i]) for row in [header, *rows]) for i in range(3)]
for name, tokens, memory in [header, *rows]:
    print(f"{name:{widths[0]}}  {tokens:>{widths[1]}}  {memory:>{widths[2]}}")

reserve full context per request             32,768  1440.0 MiB
reserve max_tokens=512 per request            2,080    91.4 MiB
paged, 16-token blocks (4 largest)              160     7.0 MiB

Saved memory becomes batch capacity. In the memory that one full-context reservation needs (8192 tokens), a paged cache fits about two hundred of these requests at roughly 40 tokens each, which is why most serving engines page their KV cache. The block size is a trade-off: bigger blocks waste more space in each sequence’s last block, and smaller ones mean longer block tables and more bookkeeping per step.

MAX Serve’s paged cache works the same way, and its attention kernels read the pages through the block table directly. Its scheduler also preempts a running request when the pool runs out of pages, and logs a message that suggests giving the cache more memory. max serve exposes the settings. --kv-cache-page-size sets the block size, 128 tokens by default. --device-memory-utilization sets the fraction of device memory the process may use, 0.95 by default, and the KV cache pool gets what is left after the weights and other allocations. The pool size and --max-batch-size together limit how many requests run at once. The handbook’s PagedAttention page describes the algorithm, and GPU memory for LLMs shows how to size the pool.

Exercises

  1. Shrink the pool to 8 blocks and rerun the paged engine. How does the engine behave when memory runs out, and what happens to time to first token?
  2. Try block sizes 4, 16, and 64. How much memory goes to partly filled last blocks in each case?
  3. This engine preempts the newest request. Change it to preempt the request that holds the most blocks instead. Which choice finishes the workload in fewer steps, and which treats long requests more fairly?

Next

Section 5.4 lets requests that share a prompt prefix share its blocks too.

5.4 Prefix caching

Real traffic repeats itself. Requests to a chat product start with the same system prompt, and every turn of a conversation resends the turns before it. Those shared tokens get the same keys and values every time, because a token’s keys and values depend only on that token and the tokens before it. Prefix caching computes them once and shares their cache blocks between requests. With the paged cache from Section 5.3, it takes little code.

Show the setup 26 lines

import time
import warnings

from minillm.prefix import PrefixCachingEngine, PrefixCachingPool
from minillm.step import Request, StepModel, compile_step
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU

config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained()
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
step = compile_step(
    load_model(config, load_checkpoint(), DEVICE, StepModel), config, DEVICE
)

Naming blocks by their content

To share a block, the engine must recognize it. A full block is identified by the tokens it holds and every token before it, since those determine its keys and values. A chained hash captures both: each block’s hash combines the previous block’s hash with the block’s own tokens. Two requests share a block exactly when their prompts agree up to the end of that block.

The pool keeps a table from hash to block, counts how many sequences use each block, and frees a block only when no sequence uses it. A block with no users whose contents are still valid stays in the table, in least-recently-used order, so a later request can still reuse it. Such blocks are evicted only when the pool runs out of free blocks.

Only full blocks are shared, and new tokens always go into a sequence’s own next block, so a shared block is never written to.

Show PrefixCachingPool, PrefixCachingEngine 100 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

from collections import OrderedDict

from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.paged import BlockPool, PagedCache, PagedEngine
from minillm.step import Request


class PrefixCachingPool(BlockPool):
    """A block pool that remembers full prompt blocks for reuse."""

    def __init__(
        self, config: LlamaConfig, num_blocks: int, block_size: int = 16
    ) -> None:
        super().__init__(config, num_blocks, block_size)
        self.refs = [0] * num_blocks
        self.by_hash: dict[int, int] = {}
        self.hash_of: dict[int, int] = {}
        self.unused: OrderedDict[int, None] = OrderedDict()  # cached, no users

    def allocate(self) -> int:
        if self.free:
            block = self.free.pop()
        elif self.unused:
            block, _ = self.unused.popitem(last=False)  # evict the oldest
            del self.by_hash[self.hash_of.pop(block)]
        else:
            raise MemoryError("the KV cache pool is full")
        self.refs[block] = 1
        return block

    def available(self) -> int:
        # Cached blocks that no request uses can be evicted and reused.
        return len(self.free) + len(self.unused)

    def release(self, blocks: list[int]) -> None:
        # Tail first: eviction then takes a prefix's last block before its
        # first, and lookup can still reuse the rest.
        for block in reversed(blocks):
            self.refs[block] -= 1
            if self.refs[block] == 0:
                if block in self.hash_of:
                    self.unused[block] = None
                else:
                    self.free.append(block)

    def block_hashes(self, tokens: list[int]) -> list[int]:
        hashes, previous = [], 0
        for start in range(
            0, len(tokens) - self.block_size + 1, self.block_size
        ):
            previous = hash(
                (previous, tuple(tokens[start : start + self.block_size]))
            )
            hashes.append(previous)
        return hashes

    def lookup(self, prompt: list[int]) -> list[int]:
        """Returns the cached blocks that start ``prompt``, and claims them.
        The last prompt token is always left to compute, since its logits
        are needed."""
        blocks = []
        for h in self.block_hashes(prompt[:-1]):
            block = self.by_hash.get(h)
            if block is None:
                break
            self.refs[block] += 1
            self.unused.pop(block, None)
            blocks.append(block)
        return blocks

    def register(self, prompt: list[int], blocks: list[int]) -> None:
        """Records a sequence's full prompt blocks for later requests."""
        # The last block may be partial; only full blocks have hashes.
        for h, block in zip(self.block_hashes(prompt), blocks, strict=False):
            if h not in self.by_hash:
                self.by_hash[h] = block
                self.hash_of[block] = h


class PrefixCachingEngine(PagedEngine):
    """A paged engine that skips computing prompt prefixes it has cached."""

    def __init__(self, *args, pool: PrefixCachingPool, **kwargs) -> None:
        super().__init__(*args, pool=pool, **kwargs)
        self.computed_prompt_tokens = 0

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: PagedCache) -> list[int]:
        tokens = (
            request.prompt + request.output
        )  # output is empty unless preempted
        cache.blocks = self.pool.lookup(tokens)
        cache.length = len(cache.blocks) * self.pool.block_size
        new = tokens[cache.length :]
        self.computed_prompt_tokens += len(new)
        return new

    def prefilled(self, request: Request, cache: PagedCache) -> None:
        self.pool.register(request.prompt, cache.blocks)

Saved to minillm/prefix.py.

prefill_tokens and prefilled are the two hooks the engine from Section 5.2 calls around each prefill. The base engine returns the whole prompt from the first and does nothing in the second. The step model already handles the rest: a prefill with a non-empty past is a step with several new tokens.

A workload with a shared prefix

Eight users ask different questions of the same assistant. Its system prompt is about 150 tokens long; real ones are often much longer:

    "You are a helpful assistant for a travel company. Answer briefly and politely. "

    "What about Lisbon?",
    "Best city for museums?",
    "Where can I hike?",
    "Is Berlin expensive?",
    "Suggest a beach town.",
]


def workload() -> list[Request]:
    now = time.perf_counter()
    return [
        Request(tokenizer([SYSTEM + q])["input_ids"][0], 8, arrived=now)
        for q in QUESTIONS
    ]


print("prompt tokens per request:", len(workload()[0].prompt))

prompt tokens per request: 163

Serve the workload twice, without and with prefix caching. The cached engine is a recording subclass that notes, for each request’s prefill, how many prompt tokens it computed and which blocks its block table holds, with their user counts:

Show serve, RecordingPrefixEngine 33 lines 
def serve(engine) -> list[Request]:
    requests = workload()
    for r in requests:
        engine.add(r)
    start = time.perf_counter()
    engine.run()
    ttft = [(r.first_token - r.arrived) * 1000 for r in requests]
    print(
        f"{type(engine).__name__:24} {time.perf_counter() - start:5.1f} s   "
        f"TTFT median {np.median(ttft):5.0f} ms, worst {max(ttft):5.0f} ms"
    )
    return requests


class RecordingPrefixEngine(PrefixCachingEngine):
    """Records each request's computed tokens and its blocks' user counts."""

    def __init__(self, *args, **kwargs) -> None:
        super().__init__(*args, **kwargs)
        self.computed, self.tables = {}, {}

    def prefill_tokens(self, request: Request, cache: object) -> list[int]:
        tokens = super().prefill_tokens(request, cache)
        self.computed[id(request)] = len(tokens)
        return tokens

    def step(self) -> None:
        super().step()
        for request, cache in self.running:
            if id(request) not in self.tables:  # just prefilled
                self.tables[id(request)] = [
                    (b, self.pool.refs[b]) for b in cache.blocks
                ]

plain = serve(
    PagedEngine(step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=BlockPool(config, 128))
)
prefix_pool = PrefixCachingPool(config, 128)
cached_engine = RecordingPrefixEngine(
    step, config, DEVICE, max_batch=4, pool=prefix_pool
)
cached = serve(cached_engine)

PagedEngine                5.1 s   TTFT median  2192 ms, worst  3499 ms
RecordingPrefixEngine      4.3 s   TTFT median  1617 ms, worst  2961 ms

total_prompt = sum(len(r.prompt) for r in cached)
print(
    f"prompt tokens computed: {cached_engine.computed_prompt_tokens} of {total_prompt} "
    f"({cached_engine.computed_prompt_tokens / total_prompt:.0%})"
)
print("same tokens:", [r.output for r in cached] == [r.output for r in plain])
assert [r.output for r in cached] == [r.output for r in plain]
assert cached_engine.computed_prompt_tokens < total_prompt

The block tables show where the reuse comes from. Each row is one request’s block table right after its prefill, each cell one block, labeled with its physical block number. Blue blocks are shared, and ×4 means four requests use the block:

tables = [cached_engine.tables[id(r)] for r in cached]
size = prefix_pool.block_size

users = np.full((len(tables), width), np.nan)
text = [[""] * width for _ in tables]
hover = [[""] * width for _ in tables]
for i, (request, table) in enumerate(zip(cached, tables)):
    for j, (block, refs) in enumerate(table):
        users[i, j] = min(refs, 2)
        text[i][j] = f"{block}<br>×{refs}" if refs > 1 else str(block)
        piece = tokenizer.decode(request.prompt[j * size : (j + 1) * size])
        hover[i][j] = (

            f"{refs} user{'s' * (refs != 1)}<br>{piece!r}"

    go.Heatmap(

        y=[f"request {i}" for i in range(len(tables))],
    )

    title="Block tables after prefill: blue blocks are shared",
    height=400,
    xaxis=dict(showgrid=False, side="top"),
    yaxis=dict(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False),
)
viz.show(fig)

The first batch computes the system prompt once per request, because nothing is cached yet: requests 0 to 3 are prefilled in the same step, so each holds its own copy of the same blocks, and only request 0’s copy enters the table. From the second batch on, each request finds the system prompt’s full blocks in the table, the blue blocks four requests share, and computes only the rest: the system prompt’s last, partly filled block plus its own question. That brings the prompt tokens computed down to 715 of 1,291, or 55%. The outputs are identical, because the reused keys and values are exactly the ones the request would have computed.

The wall-clock gain is modest here: the whole workload took about 4.3 s with prefix caching against 5.1 s without, on this page’s machine (an Apple-silicon Mac with a Metal GPU), because prefilling about 160 prompt tokens on a 135M model is only part of the workload’s time. Treat these numbers as an example: yours depend on your hardware and vary from run to run. The saving grows with the shared prefix. A multi-turn chat resends the whole conversation so far on every turn, often thousands of tokens, and an agent resends long tool descriptions on every call, so for them prefix caching removes most of the prefill.

MAX Serve enables prefix caching by default; --no-enable-prefix-caching turns it off. The same idea extends across servers: a router can send each request to the replica that already holds its prefix. The handbook covers prefix caching and inference routing.

Exercises

  1. Send the workload twice through the same engine. What fraction of prompt tokens does the second pass compute?
  2. Shrink the pool so cached blocks must be evicted. Which requests lose their prefix, and why does least-recently-used eviction suit chat traffic?
  3. Why does lookup hash prompt[:-1] instead of the whole prompt? What would break otherwise?

Next

Section 5.5 speeds up decoding instead: speculative decoding can produce several tokens per step of the large model.

5.5 Speculative decoding

Decoding produces one token per step of the model, and at small batch sizes each step is memory-bound: Section 4.3 showed that a step for several tokens costs little more than a step for one. Speculative decoding uses that spare capacity. A small, fast draft model guesses the next few tokens, and the large target model checks all the guesses in one step. Each guess the target agrees with is an extra token from that one target step, and with greedy decoding the output is exactly what the target alone would produce.

This section implements it with SmolLM2-135M drafting for SmolLM2-360M, a larger model from the same family, and measures when it pays off.

Show the setup 19 lines 
import logging
import time
import warnings

import numpy as np
import plotly.graph_objects as go
from max.pipelines import AutoTokenizer

from minillm import viz
from minillm.device import pick_device
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID, LlamaConfig
from minillm.model import load_model
from minillm.step import ContiguousCache, StepModel, compile_step, run_step
from minillm.weights import load_checkpoint

logging.getLogger("max").setLevel(logging.ERROR)
warnings.filterwarnings("ignore", message="The eager interpreter failed")
DEVICE = pick_device()  # a GPU if MAX finds one, otherwise the CPU
TARGET_ID = "HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-360M-Instruct"

Two changes to the step model

Verification needs the target’s prediction after every guessed token, not only the last, so the target’s step returns logits for all positions. When the target rejects a guess, the keys and values computed for the rejected tokens must be dropped, so the cache gets a truncate method.

Show AllLogitsStepModel, TruncatableCache, build 30 lines 
class AllLogitsStepModel(StepModel):
    def forward(self, tokens, positions, mask, past):
        x = self.model.embed_tokens(tokens)
        new_kv = []
        for layer, layer_past in zip(self.model.layers, past, strict=True):
            x, kv = layer(x, positions, mask, layer_past)
            new_kv.append(kv)
        return self.lm_head(self.model.norm(x)), new_kv


class TruncatableCache(ContiguousCache):
    def truncate(self, length: int) -> None:
        for entry in self.layers:
            entry[0], entry[1] = entry[0][:length], entry[1][:length]


def build(model_id: str):
    config = LlamaConfig.from_pretrained(model_id)
    model = load_model(
        config, load_checkpoint(model_id), DEVICE, AllLogitsStepModel
    )
    return config, compile_step(model, config, DEVICE)


draft_config, draft = build(MODEL_ID)
target_config, target = build(TARGET_ID)
print(
    f"draft: {draft_config.num_hidden_layers} layers x {draft_config.hidden_size}, "
    f"target: {target_config.num_hidden_layers} layers x {target_config.hidden_size}"
)

draft: 30 layers x 576, target: 32 layers x 960

Both models share a tokenizer, which this kind of speculative decoding requires: the target must read the draft’s token IDs as the same tokens.

The baseline: the target alone

tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(MODEL_ID)
prompt = tokenizer(["The three primary colors are"])["input_ids"][0]
N = 48


def target_alone() -> tuple[list[int], float]:
    cache, ids = TruncatableCache(target_config), list(prompt)
    start = time.perf_counter()
    logits = run_step(target, [cache], [ids], DEVICE)
    while True:
        ids.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
        if len(ids) - len(prompt) == N:
            break
        logits = run_step(target, [cache], [[ids[-1]]], DEVICE)
    return ids[len(prompt) :], time.perf_counter() - start


reference, reference_time = target_alone()
print(f"{N / reference_time:.1f} tokens/s")
print(tokenizer.decode(reference))

2.5 tokens/s
 red, blue, and yellow. These colors can be combined in various ways to create other colors. For example, by mixing red and blue, you get purple. Similarly, by mixing red and yellow, you get orange. By mixing blue

Draft, then verify

Each round:

  1. The draft model generates k tokens greedily, one step each. Its first step also feeds in any accepted tokens it hasn’t seen yet.
  2. The target runs one step over the last accepted token plus the k guesses, and returns its own greedy choice after each of them.
  3. Guesses are accepted from the left for as long as they match the target’s choice. At the first mismatch, the target’s choice replaces the guess. If all k match, the target’s choice after the last one comes free. So every round yields between 1 and k + 1 tokens.
  4. Both caches are truncated to the accepted tokens, which discards the keys and values of rejected guesses.

Because every emitted token is the target’s own greedy choice, the output is identical to the target alone. Sampling versions accept or reject each guess with a probability test that keeps the output distributed exactly as the target’s own samples would be.

Show speculative 42 lines 
def speculative(
    k: int, log: list | None = None
) -> tuple[list[int], float, int, int]:
    target_cache, draft_cache = (
        TruncatableCache(target_config),
        TruncatableCache(draft_config),
    )
    ids = list(prompt)
    rounds = accepted = 0
    start = time.perf_counter()
    logits = run_step(target, [target_cache], [ids], DEVICE)
    ids.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
    if log is not None:  # for the figure below: where each token came from
        log.append(("prefill", ids[-1]))
    while len(ids) - len(prompt) < N:
        guesses = []
        logits = run_step(
            draft, [draft_cache], [ids[draft_cache.length :]], DEVICE
        )
        for i in range(k):
            guesses.append(int(logits[0, -1].argmax()))
            if i < k - 1:
                logits = run_step(draft, [draft_cache], [[guesses[-1]]], DEVICE)
        checks = run_step(
            target,
            [target_cache],
            [ids[target_cache.length :] + guesses],
            DEVICE,
        )[0]
        choices = checks[-(k + 1) :].argmax(-1)
        n = 0
        while n < k and guesses[n] == int(choices[n]):
            n += 1
        ids += guesses[:n] + [int(choices[n])]
        if log is not None:
            log += [("accepted", t) for t in guesses[:n]]
            log.append(("bonus" if n == k else "corrected", int(choices[n])))
        rounds += 1
        accepted += n
        target_cache.truncate(len(ids) - 1)
        draft_cache.truncate(min(draft_cache.length, len(ids) - 1))
    return ids[len(prompt) :][:N], time.perf_counter() - start, rounds, accepted

print(
    f"{'k':>2} {'same output':>11} {'target steps':>12} {'accepted':>9} {'tokens/s':>9}"
)
print(f"{'-':>2} {'-':>11} {N:>12} {'-':>9} {N / reference_time:>9.1f}")
results = {}
for k in [2, 4, 6]:
    out, seconds, rounds, accepted = speculative(k)
    results[k] = (rounds, accepted)
    print(
        f"{k:>2} {str(out == reference):>11} {rounds:>12} {accepted / (rounds * k):>9.0%} {N / seconds:>9.1f}"
    )
    assert out == reference and rounds < N

 2        True           21       64%       2.2
 4        True           15       58%       1.4
 6        True           15       41%       1.2

A timeline shows where the time goes. Run the target alone and the speculative loop with k = 4 once more, and time every call to run_step. Each bar is one step, blue for the target and orange for the draft; hover over a target step in the second row to see how many tokens its round produced:

Show run_step 19 lines 
events, untimed = [], run_step


def run_step(step_fn, caches, new_tokens, device):
    start = time.perf_counter()
    logits = untimed(step_fn, caches, new_tokens, device)
    model = "draft" if step_fn is draft else "target"
    events.append((model, start, time.perf_counter(), len(new_tokens[0])))
    return logits


try:
    target_alone()
    baseline_events, events = events, []
    round_log = []
    speculative(4, round_log)
    spec_events = events
finally:
    run_step = untimed

In the second row, an orange draft step is about as wide as a blue target step, and a target step that verifies five tokens costs about the same as one that decodes a single token, as the cost model below assumes. A round spends four draft steps to earn between one and five tokens, so the steps the target saves are paid back, and more, by the draft.

When it pays off

A round costs k draft steps plus one target step. If a draft step costs a fraction \(c\) of a target step, and a round yields \(\bar{n}\) tokens on average, the speedup over the target alone is

\[\text{speedup} \approx \frac{\bar{n}}{1 + k\,c}\]

This assumes the target verifies k + 1 tokens in about the time of a one-token step. Measure \(c\) for these two models:

def ms_per_decode_step(step, config) -> float:
    cache = TruncatableCache(config)
    run_step(step, [cache], [prompt], DEVICE)
    start = time.perf_counter()
    for _ in range(10):
        run_step(step, [cache], [[42]], DEVICE)
    return (time.perf_counter() - start) / 10 * 1000


c = ms_per_decode_step(draft, draft_config) / ms_per_decode_step(
    target, target_config
)
print(f"c = {c:.2f}")
for k, (rounds, accepted) in results.items():
    tokens_per_round = (accepted + rounds) / rounds
    print(
        f"k={k}: {tokens_per_round:.2f} tokens per round -> predicted speedup {tokens_per_round / (1 + k * c):.2f}x"
    )

c = 0.69
k=2: 2.29 tokens per round -> predicted speedup 0.96x
k=4: 3.33 tokens per round -> predicted speedup 0.89x
k=6: 3.47 tokens per round -> predicted speedup 0.68x

Plot the predicted speedup against the draft’s relative cost \(c\), using the acceptance measured for each k. Above the dashed line, speculation is faster than the target alone; the dotted line marks the \(c\) of these two models:

Show the code 32 lines 
cs = np.logspace(-2, 0, 60)
fig = go.Figure()
for (k, (rounds, accepted)), color in zip(results.items(), viz.SERIES):
    per_round = (accepted + rounds) / rounds
    fig.add_trace(
        go.Scatter(
            x=cs,
            y=per_round / (1 + k * cs),
            name=f"k = {k}",
            line_color=color,
            hovertemplate=f"k = {k}, c = %{{x:.2f}}: %{{y:.2f}}x<extra></extra>",
        )
    )

fig.add_vline(
    x=c,
    line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dot"),
    annotation_text=f"these models, c = {c:.2f}",
    annotation_position="top left",

    title="Predicted speedup of speculative decoding",
    height=420,
    xaxis=dict(
        title="draft step cost / target step cost, c (log scale)",
        type="log",
        dtick=1,

viz.show(fig)

Here a draft step costs most of a target step, far more than the draft’s 2.7 times fewer parameters suggest. The two models have almost the same depth, 30 and 32 layers, and at this size much of each layer’s time is fixed per-step work that doesn’t shrink with the layer’s width. Part of that fixed work is this engine’s own: run_step packs and copies every layer’s keys and values on the host at each step, so the measured \(c\) includes that host-side packing too. So the k draft steps in a round cost more than the target steps they save, and the cost model predicts speculation at or below the speed of the target alone. The measured runs were slower still, but the conclusion is the same. A draft’s depth matters as much as its parameter count.

Speculative decoding pays off when the draft is cheap (small \(c\)), its guesses are usually accepted (large \(\bar{n}\)), and verifying k + 1 tokens costs about one target step. A cheap draft can be a 1B model drafting for a 70B target, or a small head trained on the target’s own hidden states, as in Medusa, EAGLE, and multi-token prediction (MTP). Cheap verification holds for memory-bound decoding on GPUs and less so on a busy CPU.

With \(c \approx 0.05\) and the acceptance measured above, the same code would run about two to three times as fast as the target alone, as the chart shows. MAX Serve’s speculative decoding uses drafts that read the target model’s hidden states, such as EAGLE and MTP, selected with --speculative-method. The handbook’s speculative decoding page compares the variants.

Exercises

  1. Plug different \(c\) values into the formula with the measured acceptance rates. For which \(c\) does each k break even?
  2. Change the prompt to something more predictable, such as counting from 1 to 50. How do acceptance and the best k change?
  3. Make k adaptive: increase it after a fully accepted round and decrease it after an early rejection. Does it help?

What you built

Chapter 5 is complete. Your engine schedules requests with continuous batching, stores keys and values in a paged cache, and shares cached prefixes between requests, and a separate loop speculates with a draft model for a single sequence. MAX Serve is built from the same parts, with the kernels and scheduling that make them fast on GPUs. Chapter 6 returns to the model and extends it to newer architectures.

6. Bigger models

Your SmolLM2 is a plain Llama. Newer models change a few parts, each for a reason. This chapter extends your model to them, and everything still runs on a laptop.

6.1 Qwen3: normalized queries and keys

Most open models are close relatives of the Llama architecture you built in Chapter 1. To bring up a new one, you find what changed and why. This section adds Qwen3, here the 0.6-billion-parameter model Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B. It subclasses your Llama classes, overrides only what differs, and verifies the result with the tools from Chapter 4.

Show the setup 26 lines

What changed

Compare the two configs:

                          SmolLM2      Qwen3
vocab_size                  49152     151936

rope_theta                 100000    1000000
rms_norm_eps                1e-05      1e-06

Four differences matter:

  • A larger vocabulary, 151,936 tokens, which covers many languages and code. The embedding alone holds 156M parameters, about a quarter of the model’s 596M.
  • An explicit head_dim: 16 heads of 128 dimensions make 2048, wider than the 1024-dimension hidden state. q_proj projects up, and o_proj projects back down. The config class from Section 1.3 reads head_dim when a model sets it.
  • A larger RoPE base, 1,000,000, so the rotations turn more slowly and positions stay distinguishable over longer contexts.
  • QK-norm: an RMSNorm on every query and key head, before RoPE.

The first three are config values that your code already handles. QK-norm needs new code.

QK-norm

Attention scores are dot products of queries and keys. During training, nothing stops some heads’ queries and keys from growing until a few scores dominate every softmax, which can destabilize the training of large models. Normalizing the queries and keys, with a learned scale per dimension, bounds the scores. Qwen3 and Gemma 3 normalize each head separately; OLMo 2 normalizes the whole query and key projections.

For each head’s query \(q\) (and likewise each key), with \(d\) = head_dim and a learned weight \(w\) of size \(d\):

\[\hat{q} = \frac{q}{\sqrt{\tfrac{1}{d}\sum_j q_j^2 + \epsilon}} \odot w\]

The division gives every query the same root mean square, so the length of \(\hat q\) depends on \(w\) and on \(q\)’s direction, not on \(q\)’s size. A score \(\hat q \cdot \hat k\) is then bounded however large \(q\) and \(k\) get. In code, QK-norm is two RMSNorms of size head_dim inside attention, applied to each head after the projection and before RoPE. The other classes only swap in the new attention:

Show Qwen3Attention, Qwen3DecoderLayer, Qwen3Model, Qwen3ForCausalLM 58 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

from max.graph import Dim
from max.nn import ModuleList, RMSNorm
from max.tensor import Tensor

from minillm.attention import Attention
from minillm.config import LlamaConfig
from minillm.layers import apply_rope, rope_cos_sin
from minillm.model import DecoderLayer, LlamaForCausalLM, LlamaModel


class Qwen3Attention(Attention):
    """Llama attention with an RMSNorm on each query and key head."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.q_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)
        self.k_norm = RMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)

    def project(
        self, x: Tensor, start: int | Dim | Tensor
    ) -> tuple[Tensor, Tensor, Tensor]:
        batch, seq, _ = x.shape
        q = self.q_proj(x).reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads, self.head_dim])
        k = self.k_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        v = self.v_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        q, k = self.q_norm(q), self.k_norm(k)
        cos, sin = rope_cos_sin(
            self.head_dim, self.rope_theta, start, seq, x.mesh
        )
        return apply_rope(q, cos, sin), apply_rope(k, cos, sin), v


class Qwen3DecoderLayer(DecoderLayer):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.self_attn = Qwen3Attention(config)


class Qwen3Model(LlamaModel):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.layers = ModuleList(
            [Qwen3DecoderLayer(config) for _ in range(config.num_hidden_layers)]
        )


class Qwen3ForCausalLM(LlamaForCausalLM):
    """Qwen3: a Llama model whose attention normalizes queries and keys."""

    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.model = Qwen3Model(config)

Saved to minillm/qwen3.py.

The new parameters are named q_norm and k_norm, as in Qwen3’s checkpoint, so load_model from Section 1.6 loads it unchanged. load_model builds the model inside F.lazy(), so the Llama layers that the subclasses replace are never allocated.

model = load_model(qwen, load_checkpoint(QWEN3), DEVICE, Qwen3ForCausalLM)
compiled = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)
n_params = sum(p.num_elements() for _, p in model.parameters)
print(
    f"{n_params / 1e6:.0f}M values, including the untied copy of the embedding"
)

752M values, including the untied copy of the embedding

To see what QK-norm guarantees on real activations, run layer 0’s attention on a sentence: its queries and keys, with RoPE, the scores \(q \cdot k / \sqrt{d}\), and their softmax. Then scale the layer’s input up, which scales queries and keys alike, as projection weights that grew during training would. The entropy of a softmax row measures how spread out a query’s attention is: 0 when one key takes all the weight, \(\log n\) when \(n\) keys share it equally.

Show softmax, entropy_per_head 43 lines 
attn = model.model.layers[0].self_attn
ids = AutoTokenizer(QWEN3)(
    ["Normalizing queries and keys keeps attention scores in a stable range."]
)["input_ids"][0]
seq = len(ids)
h = model.model.layers[0].input_layernorm(
    model.model.embed_tokens(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))
)
cos, sin = rope_cos_sin(attn.head_dim, attn.rope_theta, 0, seq, DEVICE)
causal = np.triu(np.full((seq, seq), -np.inf), 1)


def softmax(z: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    e = np.exp(z.astype(np.float64) - z.max(-1, keepdims=True))
    return e / e.sum(-1, keepdims=True)


def entropy_per_head(scale: float, qk_norm: bool) -> np.ndarray:
    """Mean attention entropy of each query head, in nats."""
    x = h * scale
    q = attn.q_proj(x).reshape([1, seq, attn.num_heads, attn.head_dim])
    k = attn.k_proj(x).reshape([1, seq, attn.num_kv_heads, attn.head_dim])
    if qk_norm:
        q, k = attn.q_norm(q), attn.k_norm(k)
    q = apply_rope(q, cos, sin).to_numpy()[0]
    k = apply_rope(k, cos, sin).to_numpy()[0]
    k = np.repeat(k, attn.num_heads // attn.num_kv_heads, axis=1)
    scores = np.einsum("qhd,khd->hqk", q, k) / np.sqrt(attn.head_dim)
    p = softmax(scores + causal)
    # Position 0 sees only itself, so its entropy is always 0.
    return -(p * np.log(p + 1e-30)).sum(-1)[:, 1:].mean(-1)


entropy = {
    (scale, qk_norm): entropy_per_head(scale, qk_norm)
    for scale in [1, 3, 10]
    for qk_norm in [False, True]
}
for (scale, qk_norm), e in entropy.items():
    label = "with" if qk_norm else "without"
    print(
        f"input x{scale:<3} {label:7} QK-norm: mean entropy {e.mean():.2f} nats"
    )

input x1   without QK-norm: mean entropy 1.87 nats
input x1   with    QK-norm: mean entropy 0.77 nats
input x3   without QK-norm: mean entropy 1.39 nats
input x3   with    QK-norm: mean entropy 0.77 nats
input x10  without QK-norm: mean entropy 0.18 nats
input x10  with    QK-norm: mean entropy 0.77 nats
Show the code 26 lines 
steps = [
    [
        go.Bar(
            x=np.arange(attn.num_heads),
            y=entropy[scale, qk_norm],
            name=f"{'after' if qk_norm else 'before'} QK-norm",
            marker_color=viz.BLUE if qk_norm else viz.ORANGE,
            hovertemplate="head %{x}: %{y:.2f} nats<extra>%{fullData.name}</extra>",
        )
        for qk_norm in [False, True]
    ]
    for scale in [1, 3, 10]
]
fig = go.Figure(
    layout=dict(
        title="Attention entropy per head, Qwen3 layer 0",
        height=460,
        barmode="group",
        xaxis=dict(title="query head", dtick=1),
        yaxis=dict(
            title="mean entropy (nats)",
            range=[0, 1.1 * max(e.max() for e in entropy.values())],
        ),
    )

viz.show(viz.with_slider(fig, steps, ["x1", "x3", "x10"], "input scaled "))

Move the slider. Without the norm (orange), the attention depends on the scale. Scaling the input by 10 makes queries and keys 10 times longer and the scores 100 times larger, and the softmax collapses: the orange bars fall close to 0, so each query attends to almost a single key. At x1 the orange bars sit higher than the blue ones, because Qwen3’s raw queries and keys are small; the trained model always normalizes them. The blue bars don’t move at all. After q_norm and k_norm, a query’s or key’s length depends only on its direction and the learned per-dimension scale, so no input can make a score blow up.

Verify it

Compare with Hugging Face’s PyTorch Qwen3 on a few prompts, using the metrics from Section 4.2:

tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(QWEN3)
reference = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    QWEN3, torch_dtype=torch.float32
).eval()


prompts = {
    "english": "The capital of France is",
    "code": "def add(a, b):\n    return",
    "french": "Le chat est assis sur",
}
for name, text in prompts.items():
    ids = tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0]
    ours = compiled(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()[0]
    with torch.no_grad():
        theirs = reference(torch.tensor([ids])).logits[0].numpy()
    r = compare_outputs(ours, theirs, atol=1e-3, rtol=1e-3)
    p, q = softmax(theirs), softmax(ours)
    kl = float((p * (np.log(p + 1e-30) - np.log(q + 1e-30))).sum(-1).mean())
    top1 = (ours.argmax(-1) == theirs.argmax(-1)).mean()
    print(
        f"{name:8} max err {r.max_abs_err:.1e}  KL {kl:.1e}  top-1 {top1:.0%}"
    )
    assert top1 >= 0.99 and kl < 1e-6

english  max err 1.4e-04  KL 1.0e-10  top-1 100%
code     max err 1.1e-04  KL 1.9e-11  top-1 100%
french   max err 8.5e-05  KL 7.8e-11  top-1 100%

def next_logits(ids: list[int]) -> Tensor:
    return compiled(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE))[0, -1]


prompt = tokenizer(["The three primary colors are"])["input_ids"][0]
print(
    tokenizer.decode(prompt + generate(next_logits, prompt, 24, temperature=0))
)

The three primary colors are red, blue, and yellow. The three secondary colors are red and blue, blue and yellow, and red and yellow

That is the whole bring-up: about 40 lines of model code for a new architecture, checked against the reference to rounding error. MAX’s own Qwen3 implementation serves the same model with a paged KV cache and continuous batching, the techniques that Chapter 5 builds:

max serve --model Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B --quantization-encoding float32 --no-prefer-module-v3

--no-prefer-module-v3 selects MAX’s text-generation implementation of Qwen3. By default MAX prefers an architecture’s module-based implementation (Section 7.1 explains this preference), and while the only module-based one registered under Qwen3’s architecture name is the embedding model, max serve picks it for this checkpoint and fails to start.

Qwen3 is also a reasoning model: by default it thinks step by step inside <think> tags before it answers, which costs tokens and improves answers to hard questions. Its chat template has an enable_thinking switch that turns this off. The handbook’s choosing the right model page discusses that trade-off.

  2. Remove QK-norm: copy load_model from Section 1.6, build LlamaForCausalLM instead of Qwen3ForCausalLM, and pass strict=False to load_state_dict so that it skips the q_norm and k_norm weights. Rerun the comparison. What happens to the output?
  3. Count the parameters in the embedding, the attention projections, and the MLPs. How do the proportions compare with SmolLM2’s?

Next

Section 6.2 adds sliding-window attention with Gemma 3, and Section 6.3 builds a mixture of experts.

6.2 Gemma 3: sliding-window attention

Attention’s cost grows with the square of the context, and the KV cache grows linearly with it. Gemma 3 reduces both with sliding-window attention: most of its layers look only at a recent window of tokens (512 in the 270M model), and every sixth layer looks at all of them. This section brings up Gemma 3 270M, which differs from Llama in more places than Qwen3 did in Section 6.1, and verifies it on a prompt long enough for the window to matter.

Gemma’s weights are gated: accept the license on the model page and make a Hugging Face token available (on Colab, as the HF_TOKEN secret).

What changed

Gemma 3’s config.json has fields that Llama’s doesn’t. A config subclass adds them, and from_pretrained reads every dataclass field it finds:

@dataclass(frozen=True)
class Gemma3Config(LlamaConfig):
    sliding_window: int = 512
    layer_types: tuple[str, ...] = ()
    rope_local_base_freq: float = 10000.0
    query_pre_attn_scalar: float = 256.0

    def __post_init__(self) -> None:
        super().__post_init__()
        object.__setattr__(self, "layer_types", tuple(self.layer_types))

Compared with your Llama, Gemma 3 changes six things:

PartLlamaGemma 3
Attention spanevery layer sees all past tokens5 of 6 layers see only the last 512
RoPE baseone, 100,000 for SmolLM210,000 for windowed layers, 1,000,000 for global ones
Norms per layer2, before attention and before the MLP4, before and after each
RMSNormx / rms(x) * wx / rms(x) * (1 + w) (max.nn.GemmaRMSNorm)
MLP activationSiLU (SwiGLU)tanh-approximated GELU (GeGLU)
Embeddingas storedmultiplied by \(\sqrt{\text{hidden size}}\)

It also has QK-norm, as in Qwen3, and scales attention scores by one over the square root of query_pre_attn_scalar instead of the head dimension (both are 256 in this model). Its vocabulary has 262,144 tokens, so the embedding holds 168M of the model’s 270M parameters.

Sliding-window attention

A windowed layer adds one more condition to the causal mask: query i may see key j only if \(i - j < 512\). F.band_part builds that band. Each layer reads its own RoPE base and window from the config.

The mask uses a large finite number instead of \(-\infty\), for the reason Section 5.1 gave: a query more than 512 tokens in has its whole first block of keys hidden, and a blockwise attention kernel computes \(e^{-\infty + \infty}\) = NaN for such a row.

Show sliding_window_mask, Gemma3Attention 56 lines 
def sliding_window_mask(
    q_len: int | Dim,
    kv_len: int | Dim,
    window: int,
    device: Device | DeviceMesh,
) -> Tensor:
    """Like ``causal_mask``, but each query also can't see keys ``window`` or
    more positions behind it."""
    # A finite value, not -inf: a window hides a query's whole first block of
    # keys, and a blockwise attention kernel would turn that into NaN.
    blocked = F.broadcast_to(
        Tensor(-1e30, dtype=DType.float32, device=device), [q_len, kv_len]
    )
    return F.band_part(
        blocked, num_lower=window - 1, num_upper=kv_len - q_len, exclude=True
    )


class Gemma3Attention(Attention):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config, layer: int) -> None:
        super().__init__(config)
        self.sliding = config.layer_types[layer] == "sliding_attention"
        self.window = config.sliding_window
        self.rope_theta = (
            config.rope_local_base_freq if self.sliding else config.rope_theta
        )
        self.scale = config.query_pre_attn_scalar**-0.5
        self.q_norm = GemmaRMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)
        self.k_norm = GemmaRMSNorm(self.head_dim, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        batch, seq, _ = x.shape
        q = self.q_norm(
            self.q_proj(x).reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads, self.head_dim])
        )
        k = self.k_norm(
            self.k_proj(x).reshape(
                [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
            )
        )
        v = self.v_proj(x).reshape(
            [batch, seq, self.num_kv_heads, self.head_dim]
        )
        cos, sin = rope_cos_sin(self.head_dim, self.rope_theta, 0, seq, x.mesh)
        q, k = apply_rope(q, cos, sin), apply_rope(k, cos, sin)
        if self.sliding:
            mask = sliding_window_mask(seq, seq, self.window, x.mesh)
        else:
            mask = causal_mask(seq, seq, x.mesh)
        mask = F.broadcast_to(mask, [batch, seq, seq])
        out = flash_attention(q, k, v, self.scale, mask).rebind_mapping(
            q.mapping
        )
        return self.o_proj(
            out.reshape([batch, seq, self.num_heads * self.head_dim])
        )

Saved to minillm/gemma3.py.

Compare the two masks on 16 tokens, with a window small enough to see. The slider changes the window; Gemma 3’s is 512. At a window of 16 the two panels match: once the window covers the whole sequence, a sliding layer is a global one.

    for window in [2, 4, 8, 16]
}

Once the sequence is longer than the window, a sliding layer’s cost per query stops growing, and so does the KV cache it needs: only the last window keys. That saving needs an attention kernel that skips fully masked blocks and a cache that evicts keys older than the window. The model in this section has neither: its dense mask still visits every key, and it keeps no cache, so it computes the same function without the savings. In a serving engine with both, five sliding layers for every global one mean most of Gemma 3’s KV cache stops growing at 512 tokens.

The rest of the model

GeGLU is the SwiGLU MLP with a different activation. The decoder layer has the four norms, and the model scales its embedding:

Show GeGLU, Gemma3DecoderLayer, Gemma3Model, Gemma3ForCausalLM, ... 64 lines 
class GeGLU(MLP):
    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        return self.down_proj(
            F.gelu(self.gate_proj(x), approximate="tanh") * self.up_proj(x)
        )


class Gemma3DecoderLayer(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config, layer: int) -> None:
        eps = config.rms_norm_eps
        self.input_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=eps)
        self.self_attn = Gemma3Attention(config, layer)
        self.post_attention_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(
            config.hidden_size, eps=eps
        )
        self.pre_feedforward_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(
            config.hidden_size, eps=eps
        )
        self.mlp = GeGLU(config)
        self.post_feedforward_layernorm = GemmaRMSNorm(
            config.hidden_size, eps=eps
        )

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        x = x + self.post_attention_layernorm(
            self.self_attn(self.input_layernorm(x))
        )
        return x + self.post_feedforward_layernorm(
            self.mlp(self.pre_feedforward_layernorm(x))
        )


class Gemma3Model(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config) -> None:
        self.embed_scale = math.sqrt(config.hidden_size)
        self.embed_tokens = Embedding(config.vocab_size, dim=config.hidden_size)
        self.layers = ModuleList(
            [
                Gemma3DecoderLayer(config, i)
                for i in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
            ]
        )
        self.norm = GemmaRMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)

    def forward(self, input_ids: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        x = self.embed_tokens(input_ids) * self.embed_scale
        for layer in self.layers:
            x = layer(x)
        return self.norm(x)


class Gemma3ForCausalLM(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: Gemma3Config) -> None:
        self.model = Gemma3Model(config)
        self.lm_head = Linear(config.hidden_size, config.vocab_size, bias=False)

    def forward(self, input_ids: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        return self.lm_head(self.model(input_ids))


def without_sliding_window(config: Gemma3Config) -> Gemma3Config:
    """Returns ``config`` with windows too wide to hide any token; each layer
    keeps its own RoPE base."""
    return replace(config, sliding_window=config.max_position_embeddings)

Saved to minillm/gemma3.py.

weights = load_checkpoint(GEMMA)
model = load_model(config, weights, DEVICE, Gemma3ForCausalLM)
compiled = model.compile(
    TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
)

Verify it

A short prompt never reaches the window, so verify on two prompts: a short one, and one of about 700 tokens, where windowed layers must ignore the oldest tokens.

Show softmax, verify 38 lines 
tokenizer = AutoTokenizer(GEMMA)
reference = AutoModelForCausalLM.from_pretrained(
    GEMMA, torch_dtype=torch.float32, attn_implementation="eager"
).eval()
prompts = {
    "short": "The capital of France is",
    "long (700 tokens)": "The quick brown fox jumps over the lazy dog. " * 70
    + "The capital of France is",
}


def softmax(z: np.ndarray) -> np.ndarray:
    e = np.exp(z.astype(np.float64) - z.max(-1, keepdims=True))
    return e / e.sum(-1, keepdims=True)


def verify(run) -> dict[str, tuple[np.ndarray, float]]:  # noqa: ANN001
    # Prints and returns each prompt's KL divergence per position and its
    # top-1 agreement.
    results = {}
    for name, text in prompts.items():
        ids = tokenizer([text])["input_ids"][0]
        ours = run(Tensor(np.array([ids]), device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()[0]
        with torch.no_grad():
            theirs = reference(torch.tensor([ids])).logits[0].numpy()
        r = compare_outputs(ours, theirs)
        p, q = softmax(theirs), softmax(ours)
        kl = (p * (np.log(p + 1e-30) - np.log(q + 1e-30))).sum(-1)
        top1 = (ours.argmax(-1) == theirs.argmax(-1)).mean()
        print(
            f"{name:18} {len(ids):4} tokens  max err {r.max_abs_err:.1e}  KL {kl.mean():.1e}  top-1 {top1:.0%}"
        )
        results[name] = (kl, top1)
    return results


matched = verify(compiled)
assert all(kl.mean() < 1e-6 and top1 >= 0.99 for kl, top1 in matched.values())

short                 6 tokens  max err 1.6e-04  KL 4.8e-11  top-1 100%
long (700 tokens)   706 tokens  max err 4.1e-04  KL 6.5e-12  top-1 100%

Both match. To see that the window does real work, build the same model with windows too wide to hide anything, keep each layer’s RoPE base, and verify again:

no_window = load_model(
    without_sliding_window(config), weights, DEVICE, Gemma3ForCausalLM
)
unwindowed = verify(
    no_window.compile(
        TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=DEVICE)
    )
)
long = "long (700 tokens)"
assert unwindowed["short"][0].mean() < 1e-6  # no query is far enough back
assert unwindowed[long][0].mean() > 100 * matched[long][0].mean()

short                 6 tokens  max err 1.6e-04  KL 4.8e-11  top-1 100%
long (700 tokens)   706 tokens  max err 4.9e+01  KL 2.8e-01  top-1 93%
Show the code 30 lines 
fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Scatter(
            x=np.arange(len(results[long][0])),
            y=np.maximum(results[long][0], 1e-16),
            name=name,
            mode="lines",
            line_color=color,
            hovertemplate="position %{x}: KL %{y:.1e}<extra>"
            + name
            + "</extra>",
        )
        for name, results, color in [
            ("with the window", matched, viz.BLUE),
            ("without the window", unwindowed, viz.ORANGE),
        ]
    ]
)
fig.add_vline(
    x=config.sliding_window,
    line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dot", width=1),
    annotation_text=f"position {config.sliding_window}",
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="KL divergence from the reference at each position of the long prompt",
    height=420,
    xaxis_title="position",
    yaxis=dict(title="KL (nats, log scale)", type="log", dtick=2),
)
viz.show(fig)

On the short prompt the two models are identical, because no query is more than 512 tokens from any key. On the long prompt the model without the window diverges from the reference, and the figure shows where: the two curves agree up to position 511 and split at 512, the first position with a key outside its window. The windowed layers do ignore the oldest tokens, and the bring-up must get the window right to match.

With a kernel and cache that take advantage of the window, its attention cost and KV cache stop growing at 512 tokens. With 15 of Gemma 3’s 18 layers windowed, a 32,000-token context needs about a fifth of the cache that a fully global model would need. The handbook’s GPU memory calculation page shows how cache size limits context and batch size.

Exercises

  1. Compute the KV cache size of this model for 8K and 32K tokens of context, with and without sliding windows.
  2. Plot attention weights, as in Section 1.5, for a windowed and a global layer on the long prompt. What does the window look like?
  3. Check the embedding scale: remove embed_scale and verify again. Which metric shows it?

Next

Section 6.3 builds a mixture-of-experts layer and spreads its experts across devices.

6.3 Mixture of experts

The largest open models are mixtures of experts (MoE). An MoE layer has many MLPs, the experts, instead of one, and a small router sends each token to only a few of them. The model holds the parameters of all its experts, while each token pays for only a fraction of them. This section builds an MoE layer, checks it against Hugging Face’s Qwen3-MoE implementation, and then spreads the experts across devices.

No real MoE checkpoint fits on a laptop, so this section uses Qwen3-MoE’s architecture at a small size with random weights, a common way to test large-model code. As in Section 4.1, the test compares one layer with a reference implementation.

Show the setup 32 lines

How big, and how sparse

Qwen3-30B-A3B is a typical MoE model. Its name gives its total parameters, 30 billion, and its active ones, about 3 billion:

real = json.loads(
    Path(hf_hub_download("Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B", "config.json")).read_text()
)
E, K, H, I = (
    real["num_experts"],
    real["num_experts_per_tok"],
    real["hidden_size"],
    real["moe_intermediate_size"],
)
per_expert = 3 * H * I
print(f"{E} experts per layer, {K} chosen per token")
print(
    f"expert weights per layer: {E * per_expert / 1e6:.0f}M, used per token: {K * per_expert / 1e6:.0f}M"
)

128 experts per layer, 8 chosen per token
expert weights per layer: 604M, used per token: 38M

Every token uses 8 of the 128 experts, so it touches only a sixteenth of each layer’s expert weights. Adding experts adds parameters without adding work per token. The cost moves to memory, since every expert must be loaded somewhere, which is why MoE models are usually served across several GPUs. The handbook’s parallelism strategies page covers expert parallelism, which the end of this section implements.

The layer

An MoE layer replaces the MLP. For each token:

  1. The router, a Linear from the hidden size to the number of experts, scores every expert. Softmax turns the scores into probabilities.
  2. The K most probable experts are chosen, and their probabilities are renormalized to sum to 1. Qwen3-MoE’s config asks for this with norm_topk_prob: true; a model that sets it to false weights the experts by their raw probabilities, so reusing MoE for one means dropping that step.
  3. Each chosen expert, a SwiGLU MLP like the one in Section 1.4, processes the token, and the outputs are summed, weighted by those probabilities.

In symbols, with router weights \(W_r\), the set \(\mathcal{T}\) of the \(K\) most probable experts, and expert \(e\)’s MLP \(E_e\):

\[p = \mathrm{softmax}(W_r x), \qquad y = \sum_{e \in \mathcal{T}} \frac{p_e}{\sum_{j \in \mathcal{T}} p_j} \, E_e(x)\]

The experts’ weights are stored stacked, with the expert as the first axis: one tensor for the fused gate and up projections, and one for the down projection. That is the layout of Hugging Face’s Qwen3-MoE module; the checkpoint files store each expert’s projections separately. This version computes every expert on every token and multiplies the unselected ones by zero. That wastes work, but it is simple and exact, and the last part of this section shows that it is easy to distribute.

Show Experts, MoE 66 lines 
from __future__ import annotations

from max import functional as F
from max.dtype import DType
from max.nn import Linear, Module
from max.tensor import Tensor


class Experts(Module):
    """The stacked weights of every expert: gate and up projections fused."""

    def __init__(
        self, num_experts: int, hidden: int, intermediate: int
    ) -> None:
        self.gate_up_proj = Tensor.zeros(
            [num_experts, 2 * intermediate, hidden]
        )
        self.down_proj = Tensor.zeros([num_experts, hidden, intermediate])


class MoE(Module):
    """A mixture-of-experts layer with top-k routing, as in Qwen3-MoE."""

    def __init__(
        self, num_experts: int, top_k: int, hidden: int, intermediate: int
    ) -> None:
        self.num_experts, self.top_k, self.intermediate = (
            num_experts,
            top_k,
            intermediate,
        )
        self.gate = Linear(hidden, num_experts, bias=False)  # the router
        self.experts = Experts(num_experts, hidden, intermediate)

    def routing(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """Returns each token's weight for each expert, ``[tokens, experts]``,
        zero for experts the token wasn't routed to."""
        tokens = x.shape[0]
        weights, chosen = F.top_k(F.softmax(self.gate(x)), self.top_k)
        weights = weights / weights.sum(axis=-1)
        ids = Tensor.arange(self.num_experts, dtype=DType.int64, device=x.mesh)
        one_hot = (
            chosen.reshape([tokens, self.top_k, 1])
            == ids.reshape([1, 1, self.num_experts])
        ).cast(x.dtype)
        return (
            (one_hot * weights.reshape([tokens, self.top_k, 1]))
            .sum(axis=1)
            .reshape([tokens, self.num_experts])
        )

    def forward(self, x: Tensor) -> Tensor:
        """``x`` is ``[tokens, hidden]``."""
        tokens, hidden = x.shape
        gate_up = x.reshape(
            [1, tokens, hidden]
        ) @ self.experts.gate_up_proj.transpose(1, 2)
        gate, up = (
            gate_up[:, :, : self.intermediate],
            gate_up[:, :, self.intermediate :],
        )
        outputs = (F.silu(gate) * up) @ self.experts.down_proj.transpose(
            1, 2
        )  # [E, T, H]
        weights = self.routing(x).T.reshape([self.num_experts, tokens, 1])
        return (weights * outputs).sum(axis=0).reshape([tokens, hidden])

Saved to minillm/moe.py.

Check it against Qwen3-MoE

Build Hugging Face’s Qwen3-MoE block at a small size, 8 experts with 2 per token, fill it with random weights, and load the same weights into yours. The parameter names match, so load_state_dict takes the PyTorch state directly:

torch.manual_seed(0)
small = AutoConfig.from_pretrained("Qwen/Qwen3-30B-A3B")
(
    small.hidden_size,
    small.moe_intermediate_size,
    small.num_experts,
    small.num_experts_per_tok,
) = 64, 32, 8, 2
reference = Qwen3MoeSparseMoeBlock(small).float().eval()
for p in reference.parameters():
    torch.nn.init.normal_(p, std=0.2)
state = {name: p.detach().numpy() for name, p in reference.state_dict().items()}
print({name: w.shape for name, w in state.items()})

with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    moe = MoE(num_experts=8, top_k=2, hidden=64, intermediate=32)
moe.load_state_dict(state)

x = np.random.default_rng(0).standard_normal((10, 64)).astype(np.float32)
with torch.no_grad():
    expected = reference(torch.from_numpy(x)[None])[0].numpy()
ours = moe(Tensor(x, device=DEVICE)).to_numpy()
print(f"max difference {np.abs(ours - expected).max():.1e}")
assert np.abs(ours - expected).max() < 1e-4

{'experts.gate_up_proj': (8, 64, 64), 'experts.down_proj': (8, 64, 32), 'gate.weight': (8, 64)}
max difference 3.8e-06

Where the tokens go

The routing matrix shows which experts each token uses. Count the tokens per expert for a larger batch, then make the router favor expert 0 by scaling its row of router weights, and count again:

batch = Tensor(
    np.random.default_rng(1).standard_normal((512, 64)).astype(np.float32),
    device=DEVICE,
)
weights_np = moe.routing(batch).to_numpy()
load = (weights_np > 0).sum(axis=0)
print("tokens per expert:", load, " ideal:", 512 * 2 // 8)
assert load.sum() == 512 * 2


def load_with_bias(factor: float) -> np.ndarray:
    router = state["gate.weight"].copy()
    router[0] *= factor
    with default_device(DEVICE), default_dtype(DType.float32):
        biased = MoE(num_experts=8, top_k=2, hidden=64, intermediate=32)
    biased.load_state_dict({**state, "gate.weight": router})
    return (biased.routing(batch).to_numpy() > 0).sum(axis=0)


loads = {factor: load_with_bias(factor) for factor in [1, 2, 5]}
for factor, counts in loads.items():
    print(f"expert 0's row x{factor}: tokens per expert {counts}")
assert (loads[1] == load).all()
