Every token went to exactly two experts. Move the slider to scale expert 0’s row of router weights: its scores grow, so more tokens pick it, and its bar rises to nearly twice the even split at x5 while the others shrink. How evenly a batch spreads across experts matters in practice: an expert that gets far more tokens than the others becomes the slowest part of the layer. MoE models are usually trained with a load-balancing term in the loss that pushes the router to spread tokens evenly, and serving systems monitor the load on each expert.

This dense version computes all 8 experts for every token, 4 times the work that the 2 chosen experts need. Production kernels instead group the tokens by expert and run one matrix multiplication per expert over only its tokens (a grouped GEMM), which removes the waste:

per_expert_small = 3 * 64 * 32  # gate, up, and down projections
dense = 2 * 512 * 8 * per_expert_small
sparse = 2 * 512 * 2 * per_expert_small
print(
    f"dense: {dense / 1e6:.1f} MFLOP, routed: {sparse / 1e6:.1f} MFLOP, {dense / sparse:.0f}x less"
)

dense: 50.3 MFLOP, routed: 12.6 MFLOP, 4x less

Expert parallelism

The experts are independent, so they can live on different devices. With 8 experts on 4 devices, each device holds 2. Every device receives every token, runs its own experts, and weights their outputs; the final sum over experts spans devices, which takes one all-reduce. On the simulated mesh from Section 2.1, the only change is to shard the stacked expert weights along the expert axis:

mesh = DeviceMesh((CPU(),) * 4, (4,), ("ep",))
with default_device(mesh), default_dtype(DType.float32):
    ep_moe = MoE(num_experts=8, top_k=2, hidden=64, intermediate=32)
by_expert = DeviceMapping(mesh, (Sharded(0),))
ep_moe.experts.gate_up_proj = ep_moe.experts.gate_up_proj.to(by_expert)
ep_moe.experts.down_proj = ep_moe.experts.down_proj.to(by_expert)
ep_moe.load_state_dict(state)
print(ep_moe.experts.down_proj.mapping)

with warnings.catch_warnings(record=True) as caught:
    warnings.simplefilter("always")
    with auto_reshard(mode="warn"):
        ep_out = ep_moe(Tensor(x, device=DeviceMapping(mesh, (Replicated(),))))
for w in caught:
    if "reshards" in str(w.message):
        print(w.message)
print(
    f"max difference from the reference: {np.abs(ep_out.to_numpy() - expected).max():.1e}"
)
assert np.abs(ep_out.to_numpy() - expected).max() < 1e-4

DeviceMapping(DeviceMesh(ep=4), [Sharded(axis=0)])

mul(lhs, rhs) reshards lhs
  lhs    : float32[8, 10, 1]@mesh(ep=4){"ep":R}
  rhs    : float32[8, 10, 64]@mesh(ep=4){"ep":S0}
  region : auto_reshard(DEFAULT_TRANSITIONS, mode="warn")

  decided per mesh axis
  axis    lhs           rhs           -> out        collective                          bytes    status
  "ep"    lhs{R->S0}    rhs{S0}       -> S0         local_slice(lhs)                      1.25   chosen, reshards lhs
          lhs{R}        rhs{S0->R}    -> R          allgather(rhs)                       15360
          lhs{R}        rhs{S0->S2}   -> S2         all_to_all(rhs)                      15360
          lhs{R->S1}    rhs{S0->S1}   -> S1         local_slice(lhs), all_to_all(rhs)   15361.2
          lhs{R->P}     rhs{S0->R}    -> P          keep_one_copy(lhs), allgather(rhs)  15361.2  not allowed
  result  lhs{"ep":S0}  rhs{"ep":S0}  -> {"ep":S0}

  lhs = transfer_to(lhs, DeviceMapping(mesh, (Sharded(0),)))
  with auto_reshard(mode="silent"):
  at <cell>:67 in forward()
max difference from the reference: 2.9e-06

The output matches. MAX resharded the routing weights to match the experts’ layout, so each device multiplies only its own experts’ slice, and the weighted outputs come out as partial sums that to_numpy() all-reduces.

This dense form sends every token to every device. Expert-parallel serving sends each token only to the devices that hold its chosen experts, an all-to-all exchange, and returns the results with a second one. With many experts this moves far less data, but the communication is uneven and depends on the routing. MAX serves MoE models such as GPT-OSS and DeepSeek, and supports expert parallelism across GPUs with all-to-all dispatch and combine steps.

Exercises

  1. Increase top_k to 8 (every expert). Does the output equal a weighted sum of 8 dense MLPs? What would the model lose?
  2. Implement the routed version for one device: for each expert, gather its tokens, run the MLP on them only, and scatter the results back. Check it against MoE.
  3. With the experts spread over 4 devices as above, 2 per device, how many token copies does each device process at each slider setting of the routing figure? What does the busiest device do to the time of an expert-parallel step?

7.1 Serve your architecture

In Section 1.1 you served SmolLM2 with MAX’s own implementation. This section serves yours: the LlamaForCausalLM you wrote in Section 1.6, behind the same AutoPipeline API and the same OpenAI-compatible server. MAX loads model code from outside its own package through custom architectures: a Python package that describes the model, which max serve imports with --custom-architectures.

You register the model twice. The first registration runs your model unchanged and recomputes the whole sequence at every step. The second wraps your layers in a decoder module that reads keys and values from MAX’s paged KV cache, which is how MAX’s own architectures serve.

A custom architecture package

A MAX architecture has three parts:

  • A config class tells MAX the sizes it needs to plan memory before it loads anything: the number of layers, key/value heads, and head size. It also declares the supported encodings; this model computes in float32.
  • A pipeline model loads the weights and runs the model. MAX gives it the checkpoint’s weights and, at each step, the request’s tokens, and it returns the next-token logits.
  • A SupportedArchitecture ties them together under the name in the checkpoint’s config.json, here LlamaForCausalLM, along with the tokenizer and the weight format. ARCHITECTURES lists it for --custom-architectures to find.

To keep this first version short, the pipeline model reruns the whole sequence at each step and serves one request at a time (max_batch_size is 1), like the generation loop in Section 1.7. A decoder on the paged KV cache below removes both limits.

The package is one module, minillm/serve/__init__.py, which the cells below write part by part. The first imports what the package uses from MAX:

The config class reads the sizes from the checkpoint’s Hugging Face config. ArchConfigWithStoredKVParams adds construct_kv_params, which MAX calls to size the KV cache from those sizes. llama_config converts the same config to the LlamaConfig from Section 1.3:

Show MinillmArchConfig, llama_config 38 lines 
@dataclass
class MinillmArchConfig(
    ArchConfigWithAttentionKVCache, ArchConfigWithStoredKVParams
):
    DEFAULT_ENCODING: ClassVar[SupportedEncoding] = "float32"
    SUPPORTED_ENCODINGS: ClassVar[set[SupportedEncoding]] = {"float32"}

    @property
    def num_key_value_heads(self) -> int:
        return self.huggingface_config.num_key_value_heads

    @property
    def head_dim(self) -> int:
        return self.get_head_dim(self.huggingface_config)

    @property
    def num_layers(self) -> int:
        return self.huggingface_config.num_hidden_layers

    @classmethod
    def calculate_max_seq_len(
        cls, huggingface_config: AutoConfig, model_config: MAXModelConfig
    ) -> int:
        return upper_bounded_default(
            upper_bound=huggingface_config.max_position_embeddings,
            default=model_config.max_length,
        )


def llama_config(hf: AutoConfig) -> LlamaConfig:
    """Returns the checkpoint's sizes as a ``LlamaConfig``."""
    return LlamaConfig(
        **{
            k: getattr(hf, k)
            for k in LlamaConfig.__dataclass_fields__
            if hasattr(hf, k)
        }
    )

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

The pipeline model’s main job is to build your model. build_model casts the checkpoint’s bfloat16 weights to float32, builds your LlamaForCausalLM with load_model from Section 1.6, and compiles it for any sequence length:

def build_model(
    config: LlamaConfig,
    pipeline_config: PipelineConfig,
    weights: Weights,
    adapter: WeightsAdapter | None,
    device: Device,
) -> Callable[[Tensor], Tensor]:
    """Builds and compiles minillm's ``LlamaForCausalLM`` from a checkpoint."""
    state = parse_state_dict_from_weights(pipeline_config, weights, adapter)
    state = {name: w.astype(DType.float32) for name, w in state.items()}
    model = load_model(config, state, device)
    return model.compile(
        TensorType(DType.int64, ("batch", "seq_len"), device=device)
    )

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

MAX constructs the pipeline model with a long list of arguments, which the constructor passes through before it calls build_model. model_config_cls names the config class, which MAX asks to size the KV cache. prepare_initial_token_inputs turns the request’s context into model inputs: here all of its tokens, since the model keeps no cache. execute runs the compiled model and returns the last position’s logits:

Show TokenInputs, MinillmPipelineModel 41 lines 
@dataclass
class TokenInputs(ModelInputs):
    tokens: Buffer

    @property
    def buffers(self) -> tuple[Buffer, ...]:
        return (self.tokens,)


class MinillmPipelineModel(PipelineModelWithKVCache[TextContext]):
    model_config_cls: ClassVar[type[MinillmArchConfig]] = MinillmArchConfig

    def __init__(self, *args: object, **kwargs: object) -> None:
        super().__init__(*args, **kwargs)
        self.model = build_model(
            llama_config(self.huggingface_config),
            self.pipeline_config,
            self.weights,
            self.adapter,
            self.devices[0],
        )

    def prepare_initial_token_inputs(
        self,
        replica_batches: Sequence[Sequence[TextContext]],
        kv_cache_inputs: KVCacheInputs[Buffer, Buffer] | None = None,
        return_n_logits: int = 1,
    ) -> TokenInputs:
        ids = replica_batches[0][0].tokens.all
        tokens = np.array([ids], dtype=np.int64)
        inputs = TokenInputs(Buffer.from_numpy(tokens).to(self.devices[0]))
        inputs.kv_cache_inputs = kv_cache_inputs
        return inputs

    def execute(self, model_inputs: ModelInputs) -> ModelOutputs:
        assert isinstance(model_inputs, TokenInputs)
        logits = self.model(Tensor.from_dlpack(model_inputs.tokens))
        last = Buffer.from_numpy(
            np.ascontiguousarray(logits.to_numpy()[0, -1:, :])
        ).to(self.devices[0])
        return ModelOutputs(logits=last, next_token_logits=last)

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

Finally, SupportedArchitecture registers the parts under the checkpoint’s architecture name. required_arguments makes MAX serve one request at a time and turns off prefix caching, which this model can’t use:

def tie_embeddings(state_dict: dict[str, Weights], **_: object) -> dict:
    """Loads the checkpoint as is, with the LM head sharing the embedding."""
    weights = {name: w.data() for name, w in state_dict.items()}
    weights.setdefault("lm_head.weight", weights["model.embed_tokens.weight"])
    return weights


minillm_arch = SupportedArchitecture(
    name="LlamaForCausalLM",
    task=PipelineTask.TEXT_GENERATION,
    example_repo_ids=["HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct"],
    default_weights_format=WeightsFormat.safetensors,
    default_encoding=MinillmArchConfig.DEFAULT_ENCODING,
    supported_encodings=MinillmArchConfig.SUPPORTED_ENCODINGS,
    pipeline_model=MinillmPipelineModel,
    tokenizer=TextTokenizer,
    context_type=TextContext,
    multi_gpu_supported=False,
    weight_adapters={WeightsFormat.safetensors: tie_embeddings},
    config=MinillmArchConfig,
    required_arguments={"enable_prefix_caching": False, "max_batch_size": 1},
    supports_overlap_scheduler=False,
    supports_device_graph_capture=False,
)

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

Use it in Python

custom_architectures imports the package and registers its architectures, as --custom-architectures does for the CLI. A registered architecture with the same name as one of MAX’s replaces it in this process. MAX registers module-based implementations under the name with a _ModuleV3 suffix and prefers them, so prefer_module_v3=False selects the LlamaForCausalLM registered above:

ours = AutoPipeline(
    MODEL_ID,
    quantization_encoding="float32",
    custom_architectures=["minillm.serve"],
    prefer_module_v3=False,
)
result = ours(PROMPT, max_new_tokens=16, top_k=1)
ours.close()
print(result)
print("same as MAX's implementation:", result == expected)
assert result == expected

['The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department']
same as MAX's implementation: True

The pipeline model is yours, and its greedy output matches MAX’s implementation token for token.

A decoder on the paged KV cache

The pipeline model above recomputes every token at every step and runs one request at a time. MAX’s own architectures don’t write a pipeline model. They register a decoder module for the DECODER role, and MAX builds the pipeline model around it. That pipeline model brings what Chapter 5 built by hand: continuous batching, a paged KV cache, and prefix caching.

At each step, the decoder’s forward receives a DecoderInputs:

  • tokens: the new tokens of every request in the batch, concatenated into one ragged sequence. A request’s prompt arrives whole at its first step, and then one token per step.
  • input_row_offsets: where each request starts in tokens, plus the total length at the end.
  • kv: the paged KV cache. per_device_caches unpacks it into one PagedCacheValues per device.

Your embedding, norms, and MLP work on ragged tokens unchanged, since they treat each token on its own. Only attention needs the cache, and two MAX kernels supply it. rope_split_store_ragged splits the fused Q/K/V projection, rotates the queries and keys, and writes the keys and values into the cache’s pages. flash_attention_ragged then attends each new query to its own request’s cached keys, with a causal mask. The kernels take the RoPE table packed as (cos, sin) pairs, which rope_table builds from your rope_cos_sin from Section 1.4:

Show rope_table, PagedAttention 59 lines 
def rope_table(config: LlamaConfig, length: int, device: Device) -> Tensor:
    """Returns the RoPE table as ``[length, head_dim]`` (cos, sin) pairs."""
    cos, sin = rope_cos_sin(
        config.head_dim, config.rope_theta, 0, length, device
    )
    half = config.head_dim // 2
    pairs = F.stack([cos[:, :half], sin[:, :half]], axis=-1)
    return pairs.reshape([length, config.head_dim])


class PagedAttention(Module):
    """Grouped-query attention that reads keys and values from the cache."""

    def __init__(
        self, config: LlamaConfig, kv_params: KVCacheParams, layer_idx: int
    ) -> None:
        q_dim = config.num_attention_heads * config.head_dim
        kv_dim = config.num_key_value_heads * config.head_dim
        self.q_proj = Linear(config.hidden_size, q_dim, bias=False)
        self.k_proj = Linear(config.hidden_size, kv_dim, bias=False)
        self.v_proj = Linear(config.hidden_size, kv_dim, bias=False)
        self.o_proj = Linear(q_dim, config.hidden_size, bias=False)
        self.n_heads = config.num_attention_heads
        self.head_dim = config.head_dim
        self.kv_params = kv_params
        self.layer_idx = layer_idx

    def forward(
        self,
        x: Tensor,
        kv: PagedCacheValues,
        rope: Tensor,
        input_row_offsets: Tensor,
    ) -> Tensor:
        qkv = F.concat(
            [self.q_proj(x), self.k_proj(x), self.v_proj(x)], axis=-1
        )
        # The kernels read the layer index on the host.
        layer = F.constant(self.layer_idx, DType.uint32, device=CPU())
        q = rope_split_store_ragged(
            kv_params=self.kv_params,
            qkv=qkv,
            input_row_offsets=input_row_offsets,
            freqs_cis=rope,
            kv_collection=kv,
            layer_idx=layer,
            n_heads=self.n_heads,
            interleaved=False,
        )
        out = flash_attention_ragged(
            self.kv_params,
            input=q.reshape([-1, self.n_heads, self.head_dim]),
            input_row_offsets=input_row_offsets,
            kv_collection=kv,
            layer_idx=layer,
            mask_variant=MHAMaskVariant.CAUSAL_MASK,
            scale=self.head_dim**-0.5,
        )
        return self.o_proj(out.reshape([-1, self.n_heads * self.head_dim]))

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

The layer, the model, and the decoder mirror DecoderLayer, LlamaModel, and LlamaForCausalLM from Section 1.6, with the same attribute names, so the checkpoint loads into them unchanged. MAX constructs the decoder as MinillmDecoder(config) from the config class. The decoder returns the logits of each request’s last token, the one its next token follows:

Show PagedDecoderLayer, PagedLlamaModel, MinillmDecoder 55 lines 
class PagedDecoderLayer(Module):
    def __init__(
        self, config: LlamaConfig, kv_params: KVCacheParams, layer_idx: int
    ) -> None:
        eps = config.rms_norm_eps
        self.input_layernorm = RMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=eps)
        self.self_attn = PagedAttention(config, kv_params, layer_idx)
        self.post_attention_layernorm = RMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=eps)
        self.mlp = MLP(config)

    def forward(
        self,
        x: Tensor,
        kv: PagedCacheValues,
        rope: Tensor,
        input_row_offsets: Tensor,
    ) -> Tensor:
        h = self.input_layernorm(x)
        x = x + self.self_attn(h, kv, rope, input_row_offsets)
        return x + self.mlp(self.post_attention_layernorm(x))


class PagedLlamaModel(Module):
    def __init__(self, config: LlamaConfig, kv_params: KVCacheParams) -> None:
        self.embed_tokens = Embedding(config.vocab_size, dim=config.hidden_size)
        self.layers = ModuleList(
            [
                PagedDecoderLayer(config, kv_params, i)
                for i in range(config.num_hidden_layers)
            ]
        )
        self.norm = RMSNorm(config.hidden_size, eps=config.rms_norm_eps)


class MinillmDecoder(Module[[DecoderInputs], DecoderOutputs]):
    def __init__(self, arch_config: MinillmArchConfig) -> None:
        self.config = llama_config(arch_config.huggingface_config)
        self.max_seq_len = arch_config.max_seq_len
        self.kv_params = arch_config.get_kv_params()
        self.model = PagedLlamaModel(self.config, self.kv_params)
        self.lm_head = Linear(
            self.config.hidden_size, self.config.vocab_size, bias=False
        )

    def forward(self, inputs: DecoderInputs) -> DecoderOutputs:
        (kv,) = per_device_caches(self.kv_params, inputs.kv)
        offsets = inputs.input_row_offsets
        rope = rope_table(self.config, self.max_seq_len, inputs.tokens.device)
        x = self.model.embed_tokens(inputs.tokens)
        for layer in self.model.layers:
            x = layer(x, kv, rope, offsets)
        last = F.gather(x, offsets[1:] - 1, axis=0)
        return DecoderOutputs(
            next_token_logits=self.lm_head(self.model.norm(last))
        )

Saved to minillm/serve/__init__.py.

The registration names the decoder under modules instead of a pipeline model. Its name, LlamaForCausalLM_ModuleV3, replaces MAX’s own module-based Llama, so MAX selects it by default:

minillm_decoder_arch = SupportedArchitecture(
    name="LlamaForCausalLM_ModuleV3",
    task=PipelineTask.TEXT_GENERATION,
    example_repo_ids=["HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct"],
    default_weights_format=WeightsFormat.safetensors,
    default_encoding=MinillmArchConfig.DEFAULT_ENCODING,
    supported_encodings=MinillmArchConfig.SUPPORTED_ENCODINGS,
    modules={GraphRole.DECODER: MinillmDecoder},
    tokenizer=TextTokenizer,
    context_type=TextContext,
    weight_adapters={WeightsFormat.safetensors: tie_embeddings},
    config=MinillmArchConfig,
    supports_overlap_scheduler=False,
    supports_device_graph_capture=False,
)
ARCHITECTURES = [minillm_arch, minillm_decoder_arch]

Serve it

The same package serves over HTTP. --custom-architectures minillm.serve imports it, and MAX serves the decoder:

start = time.perf_counter()
server = start_max_serve(
    MODEL_ID,
    "--quantization-encoding",
    "float32",
    "--custom-architectures",
    "minillm.serve",
)
print(f"server ready in {time.perf_counter() - start:.0f} s")

client = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")
completion = client.completions.create(
    model=MODEL_ID, prompt=PROMPT[0], max_tokens=16, temperature=0
)
print(PROMPT[0] + completion.choices[0].text)
assert PROMPT[0] + completion.choices[0].text == expected[0]
server.terminate()
_ = server.wait()

server ready in 20 s
The capital of France is Paris. Paris is the largest city in France and the capital of the French department

Your model now answers an OpenAI client exactly as MAX’s implementation does, on MAX’s paged KV cache and scheduler. The handbook’s OpenAI-compatible API page covers the request format. MAX’s guide to serving custom model architectures covers the rest of a production architecture, such as weight adapters for other checkpoint formats and multi-GPU serving.

Exercises

  1. Register Qwen3ForCausalLM from Section 6.1 as a decoder, and serve Qwen/Qwen3-0.6B through it. rope_split_store_ragged takes q_norm_weight, k_norm_weight, and rms_norm_eps for Qwen3’s per-head norms. Which parts of the package depend on the model class, and which work unchanged?
  2. Serve each registration in turn, --no-prefer-module-v3 selecting the first, and measure both with max benchmark from Section 7.2. How does throughput change as you raise --max-concurrency?
  3. rope_table rebuilds the RoPE table at every step. Build it once in MinillmDecoder.__init__ instead. Why does MAX then report the table as a weight missing from the checkpoint, and how could the weight adapter supply it?

Next

Section 7.2 measures the server under load with max benchmark.

7.2 Benchmark a server

A server that answers one request correctly can still fall over under real traffic. max benchmark measures a running server the way users experience it: it sends many requests, some at the same time, and records how long each one waits for its first token and for each token after that. This section benchmarks MAX’s SmolLM2 and the architecture you served in Section 7.1, and explains the numbers.

Show the setup 15 lines 
import json
import subprocess
import tempfile
from collections.abc import Iterator
from contextlib import contextmanager
from pathlib import Path

import plotly.graph_objects as go
from plotly.subplots import make_subplots

from minillm import viz
from minillm.config import MODEL_ID
from minillm.server import start_max_serve

WORKDIR = Path(tempfile.mkdtemp())

What to measure

These numbers describe a serving run. The handbook’s LLM inference metrics page defines them in detail:

  • Time to first token (TTFT): the time from sending a request to receiving its first token. It includes the wait in the queue and the prefill. Users notice it as the delay before the answer starts.
  • Time per output token (TPOT): the average gap between later tokens, one decode step. Users notice it as the reading speed.
  • Requests per second and output tokens per second: the throughput of the whole server, which decides how many users one GPU can serve.

Concurrency, the number of requests in flight at once, connects them. More concurrency lets the server batch more requests per step (Chapter 5), which raises throughput, but each request can then wait longer.

Run a benchmark

From a terminal, a benchmark is one command against a running server. This one sends 32 requests of 128 random input tokens each, asks for 32 output tokens per request, and keeps at most 8 in flight:

max benchmark --model HuggingFaceTB/SmolLM2-135M-Instruct --dataset-name random \
  --random-input-len 128 --random-output-len 32 \
  --num-prompts 32 --max-concurrency 8 --result-filename result.json

The random dataset gives every run the same shape, which suits comparisons. The default dataset, sharegpt, replays real chat conversations with varied lengths. The helpers below start a server, run a benchmark from Python with the same flags, and read the result file, a JSON object with the summary metrics (request_throughput, mean_ttft_ms, p95_ttft_ms, and so on) and one record per request (request_records):

Show server, benchmark, summary, show 67 lines 
@contextmanager
def server(*flags: str) -> Iterator[None]:
    process = start_max_serve(
        MODEL_ID, "--quantization-encoding", "float32", *flags
    )
    try:
        yield
    finally:
        process.terminate()
        process.wait()


def benchmark(concurrency: int, *count: str) -> dict:
    """Runs ``max benchmark`` and returns its result file. ``count`` is
    ``--num-prompts N`` or ``--num-prompts-multiplier M``."""
    result = WORKDIR / f"result_{len(list(WORKDIR.glob('*.json')))}.json"
    subprocess.run(
        [
            "max",
            "benchmark",
            "--model",
            MODEL_ID,
            "--port",
            "8000",
            "--dataset-name",
            "random",
            "--random-input-len",
            "128",
            "--random-output-len",
            "32",
            "--max-concurrency",
            str(concurrency),
            *count,
            "--result-filename",
            str(result),
            "--log-dir",
            str(WORKDIR / "logs"),
        ],
        check=True,
        stdout=subprocess.DEVNULL,
        stderr=subprocess.DEVNULL,
    )
    return json.loads(result.read_text())


def summary(run: dict) -> dict[str, float]:
    return {
        "concurrency": run["max_concurrency"],
        "requests/s": run["request_throughput"],
        "output tokens/s": run["total_output_tokens"] / run["duration"],
        "TTFT (ms)": run["mean_ttft_ms"],
        "TPOT (ms)": run["mean_tpot_ms"],
    }


def show(runs: list[dict]) -> None:
    rows = [summary(run) for run in runs]
    print("".join(f"{name:>17}" for name in rows[0]))
    for row in rows:
        print(
            "".join(
                f"{value:>17.1f}"
                if isinstance(value, float)
                else f"{value:>17}"
                for value in row.values()
            )
        )

MAX’s SmolLM2

with server():
    max_runs = [
        benchmark(1, "--num-prompts", "16"),
        benchmark(8, "--num-prompts", "32"),
    ]
show(max_runs)

      concurrency       requests/s  output tokens/s        TTFT (ms)        TPOT (ms)
                1              1.5             46.6             85.4             19.4
                8              8.5            271.8            236.0             22.7

With 8 requests in flight, the server batches them into each decode step. Throughput rises several times, while TPOT barely changes: one decode step costs about the same for 8 sequences as for 1. At this model size, most of a step’s cost is fixed work, such as reading the weights and launching kernels, and little of it is arithmetic per sequence. The handbook’s batching page explains why. TTFT rises because requests that arrive together are prefilled together, and a new request waits for the current step to finish.

The concurrency-8 run sends more requests because max benchmark doesn’t count all of them. A run this short reports over a trimmed window: at concurrency above 1 it leaves out the first max_concurrency requests sent and the last max_concurrency completed, because the server is still filling up or draining while they run, and divides by the time from the first counted request’s submission to the last one’s completion. With at least 100 requests at concurrency above 1, it first looks for a steady-state window automatically and trims only when it finds none. Either way, the result file says which requests counted (measured in each request record).

Your architecture

Now run the same benchmark against the package from Section 7.1:

with server("--custom-architectures", "minillm.serve", "--no-prefer-module-v3"):
    ours = [
        benchmark(1, "--num-prompts", "8"),
        benchmark(8, "--num-prompts", "24"),
    ]  # fewer: this server is slow
show(ours)

      concurrency       requests/s  output tokens/s        TTFT (ms)        TPOT (ms)
                1              0.5             14.7             82.3             67.7
                8              0.2              6.5          17743.1             94.3

def gain(runs: list[dict]) -> float:
    return runs[1]["request_throughput"] / runs[0]["request_throughput"]


def gain_all_requests(runs: list[dict]) -> float:
    rates = []
    for run in runs:
        records = run["request_records"]
        start = min(r["submit_time"] for r in records)
        end = max(r["complete_time"] for r in records)
        rates.append(len(records) / (end - start))
    return rates[1] / rates[0]


print(
    f"throughput gain from concurrency 8: MAX's {gain(max_runs):.1f}x, "
    f"yours {gain(ours):.1f}x"
)
print(
    f"over every request, not just the counted ones: "
    f"MAX's {gain_all_requests(max_runs):.1f}x, "
    f"yours {gain_all_requests(ours):.1f}x"
)
assert gain(max_runs) > 1.5 and gain_all_requests(ours) < 1.5

throughput gain from concurrency 8: MAX's 5.8x, yours 0.4x
over every request, not just the counted ones: MAX's 5.2x, yours 0.7x

metrics = ["requests/s", "output tokens/s", "TTFT (ms)", "TPOT (ms)"]
fig = make_subplots(
    rows=1, cols=4, horizontal_spacing=0.07, subplot_titles=metrics
)
for name, runs, color in [
    ("MAX's SmolLM2", max_runs, viz.BLUE),
    ("yours (Section 7.1)", ours, viz.ORANGE),
]:
    rows = [summary(run) for run in runs]
    for col, metric in enumerate(metrics, start=1):
        fig.add_trace(
            go.Bar(
                x=[f"c = {r['concurrency']}" for r in rows],
                y=[r[metric] for r in rows],
                name=name,
                marker_color=color,
                legendgroup=name,
                showlegend=col == 1,
                hovertemplate=f"{name}, %{{x}}: %{{y:,.1f}} {metric}<extra></extra>",
            ),
            row=1,
            col=col,
        )
fig.update_yaxes(type="log", title_text="ms (log scale)", row=1, col=3)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Serving SmolLM2 at concurrency 1 and 8",
    barmode="group",
    height=440,
    legend=dict(y=-0.12, yanchor="top"),
    margin=dict(t=96),
)
viz.show(fig)

The two servers differ in two ways, and earlier sections explain both:

  • TPOT is several times higher. The pipeline model from Section 7.1 reruns the whole sequence for every token, as Section 1.7 did, so a step grows with the sequence. The KV cache from Section 1.8 makes it constant.
  • Concurrency doesn’t raise throughput. The package declares max_batch_size 1, so the server runs one request at a time and queues the rest. The queue shows up in TTFT: a request waits for the ones ahead of it to finish completely.

The reported throughput even drops, to half the concurrency-1 rate or less, while counted over every request of the run it drops much less. The trim explains the difference. Here is every request of your server’s concurrency-8 run, from the result file’s request records. The shaded span is the window that max benchmark divides by:

Show the code 56 lines 
records = sorted(ours[1]["request_records"], key=lambda r: r["submit_time"])
start = records[0]["submit_time"]
counted = [r for r in records if r["measured"]]
window = (
    min(r["submit_time"] for r in counted) - start,
    max(r["complete_time"] for r in counted) - start,
)
rows = [
    f"request {i}" + ("" if r["measured"] else " (not counted)")
    for i, r in enumerate(records)
]
fig = go.Figure(
    [
        go.Bar(
            y=rows,
            x=[r["ttft_ms"] / 1000 for r in records],
            base=[r["submit_time"] - start for r in records],
            orientation="h",
            name="waiting, then prefill",
            marker_color=viz.MUTED,
            hovertemplate="%{y}: first token after %{x:.1f} s<extra></extra>",
        ),
        go.Bar(
            y=rows,
            x=[(r["latency_ms"] - r["ttft_ms"]) / 1000 for r in records],
            base=[
                r["submit_time"] - start + r["ttft_ms"] / 1000 for r in records
            ],
            orientation="h",
            name="generating",
            marker_color=[
                viz.ORANGE if r["measured"] else viz.GRID for r in records
            ],
            hovertemplate="%{y}: generating for %{x:.1f} s<extra></extra>",
        ),
    ]
)
fig.add_vrect(
    x0=window[0],
    x1=window[1],
    fillcolor=viz.ORANGE,
    opacity=0.08,
    line_width=0,
    annotation_text=(
        f"counted: {len(counted)} requests in {window[1] - window[0]:.0f} s"
    ),
    annotation_position="top left",
)
fig.update_layout(
    title="Your server at concurrency 8: every request of the run",
    barmode="overlay",
    height=560,
    xaxis_title="seconds since the first request was sent",
    yaxis=dict(autorange="reversed", showgrid=False),
)
viz.show(fig)

The first 8 requests are sent at once and finish one after another. Each finished request frees a client slot, so the counted requests, 8 to 15, are sent while 7 earlier requests still wait in the server’s queue. Their window also holds the service time of those 7 uncounted requests: about 15 requests’ work, credited to 8. Over every request, from the first submission to the last completion, throughput stays close to the concurrency-1 rate, as it should for a server that runs one request at a time. The trim suits a server that batches, where the ramp-up and the drain distort the numbers. For a server that serializes requests, read the untrimmed rate, or pass --skip-first-n-requests 0 --skip-last-n-requests 0.

The engine in Chapter 5 fixed both with a paged KV cache and continuous batching. MAX’s own architectures get them from MAX’s serving stack, which is why a production architecture registers a decoder on MAX’s paged KV cache, as the second registration in Section 7.1 does.

Reading benchmarks well

Measure a server at several concurrency levels, not one, and plot latency against throughput. --max-concurrency 1,2,4,8,16,32 runs such a sweep in one command. Pair it with --num-prompts-multiplier 8 instead of a fixed --num-prompts: each level then sends 8 requests per slot, so the trim never uses up a level’s requests, as a fixed 32 would at concurrency 32. The cell below runs the levels one at a time with the same flag, against MAX’s SmolLM2:

with server():
    benchmark(1, "--num-prompts", "2")  # warm up the new server
    sweep = [
        benchmark(c, "--num-prompts-multiplier", "8")
        for c in [1, 2, 4, 8, 16, 32]
    ]
show(sweep)

      concurrency       requests/s  output tokens/s        TTFT (ms)        TPOT (ms)
                1              1.4             45.3            109.9             19.2
                2              2.8             88.0             95.6             20.4
                4              4.8            153.1            148.1             22.2
                8              8.3            265.0            280.8             22.1
               16             10.8            346.3            609.3             24.2
               32             14.2            455.9            999.4             33.3
Show the code 55 lines 
TARGET_MS = 500
rows = [summary(run) for run in sweep]
throughput = [r["output tokens/s"] for r in rows]
p95_ttft = [run["p95_ttft_ms"] for run in sweep]
meets = [t <= TARGET_MS for t in p95_ttft]
best = max(
    (i for i, ok in enumerate(meets) if ok),
    key=lambda i: throughput[i],
    default=None,
)
fig = go.Figure(
    go.Scatter(
        x=throughput,
        y=p95_ttft,
        mode="lines+markers+text",
        text=[f"c = {r['concurrency']}" for r in rows],
        textposition="top left",
        marker=dict(
            size=10, color=[viz.BLUE if ok else viz.RED for ok in meets]
        ),
        line=dict(color=viz.MUTED),
        customdata=[
            [r["concurrency"], run["mean_tpot_ms"]]
            for r, run in zip(rows, sweep)
        ],
        hovertemplate="concurrency %{customdata[0]}: %{x:.0f} tokens/s, "
        "p95 TTFT %{y:.0f} ms, mean TPOT %{customdata[1]:.1f} ms<extra></extra>",
    )
)
fig.add_hline(
    y=TARGET_MS,
    line=dict(color=viz.INK_SECONDARY, dash="dash"),
    annotation_text=f"target: p95 TTFT {TARGET_MS} ms",
    annotation_position="top left",
)
if best is not None:
    fig.add_annotation(
        x=throughput[best],
        y=p95_ttft[best],
        text="highest throughput<br>within the target",
        showarrow=True,
        arrowcolor=viz.INK_SECONDARY,
        ax=40,
        ay=40,
    )
fig.update_layout(
    title="MAX's SmolLM2: latency against throughput as concurrency rises",
    height=440,
    showlegend=False,
    xaxis=dict(title="output tokens/s", rangemode="tozero"),
    yaxis=dict(
        title="p95 time to first token (ms, log scale)", type="log", dtick=1
    ),
)
viz.show(fig)

Each point is one concurrency level, and red points miss a target of a 95th-percentile TTFT under 500 ms. Throughput keeps rising with concurrency, but TTFT rises faster once the batch fills. In the run on this page, the highest throughput within the target came at concurrency 8, and concurrency 16 added about a third more throughput at more than twice the p95 TTFT. The levels at 16 and 32 send 128 and 256 requests, enough for max benchmark’s steady-state window; the others are trimmed. Your numbers depend on your hardware.

  • Benchmark the workload you will serve. Input and output lengths change the results a lot: long prompts stress prefill and TTFT, and long answers stress decode and TPOT.
  • Sweep concurrency, as above, with --num-prompts-multiplier rather than a fixed --num-prompts. Throughput grows ever more slowly once the hardware is busy, while latency keeps rising.
  • Set a latency target first, such as a TTFT under 500 ms at the 95th percentile, then find the highest throughput that meets it. The result file stores percentiles (p95_ttft_ms, p99_tpot_ms, and so on) next to the means, and --goodput ttft:500 tpot:50 adds the share of requests that met every target (goodput_attainment).

The handbook’s LLM performance benchmarks page covers benchmark design, and max benchmark --help lists the other datasets and options.

Exercises

  1. Extend the sweep of MAX’s SmolLM2 to concurrency 64 and 128 with --num-prompts-multiplier 4 and --goodput ttft:500. Where does throughput stop growing, and at which level does goodput_attainment fall below 90%?
  2. Repeat the MAX run with --random-input-len 1024. Which metric changes most, and why?
  3. Do the second exercise of Section 7.1, raise max_batch_size in its package to 4, and benchmark it again at concurrency 8.

Next

Section 7.3 constrains what the server generates: JSON that follows a schema, and calls to your own functions.

7.3 Structured output and tools

A chat answer is for people. A program needs output it can parse: a JSON object with the right fields, or the name of a function to call with its arguments. Instructions in the prompt don’t get that reliably, least of all from a small model. This section makes the server guarantee the format with structured output, then builds function calling on top of it.

Show the imports 7 lines 
import json

from openai import OpenAI
from pydantic import BaseModel, ConfigDict, Field

from minillm.config import MODEL_ID
from minillm.server import start_max_serve

Structured output is off by default, and --enable-structured-output turns it on:

server = start_max_serve(
    MODEL_ID,
    "--quantization-encoding",
    "float32",
    "--enable-structured-output",
)
client = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")


def chat(messages: list[dict[str, str]], **options: object) -> str:
    completion = client.chat.completions.create(
        model=MODEL_ID,
        messages=messages,
        max_tokens=100,
        temperature=0,
        **options,
    )
    return completion.choices[0].message.content

JSON that follows a schema

Without a constraint, the model answers in prose:

question = [
    {
        "role": "user",
        "content": "Tell me about Rome: its country and three landmarks.",
    }
]
print(chat(question))

Rome, the Eternal City, is a city that has witnessed so much history and transformation. As a helpful AI assistant, I'm here to share with you the fascinating facts about Rome, its country, and three iconic landmarks.

Rome, the Eternal City, is a city that has witnessed so much history and transformation. It's a place where ancient civilizations like the Romans, Greeks, and Etruscans have left their mark, and it's a place where the Romans have built a lasting

Describe the answer you want as a Pydantic model instead. Pydantic turns it into a JSON schema, which the request passes as response_format:

class City(BaseModel):
    model_config = ConfigDict(extra="forbid")  # no fields beyond these
    name: str
    country: str
    landmarks: list[str] = Field(min_length=3, max_length=3)


schema = {
    "type": "json_schema",
    "json_schema": {"name": "City", "schema": City.model_json_schema()},
}
answer = chat(question, response_format=schema)
print(answer)
city = City.model_validate_json(answer)
city

{"name":"Rome","country":"Italy","landmarks":["The Colosseum","The Roman Forum","The Pantheon"]}
City(name='Rome', country='Italy', landmarks=['The Colosseum', 'The Roman Forum', 'The Pantheon'])

model_validate_json raises if the text doesn’t match the schema. With structured output, the text always matches.

How the server guarantees it

The guarantee comes from the sampler. MAX compiles the schema into a grammar, and at every decode step the grammar says which tokens may come next: after {"name": "Rome", , only tokens that continue toward "country". MAX masks every other token out of the logits before sampling, as the top-p filter in Section 1.7 did with \(-\infty\), so the model can only pick a token that keeps the output valid. Generation ends when the grammar is complete.

The schema fixes the form of the answer and leaves the facts to the model. The model can skip any requirement that the schema leaves out: without min_length, a small model may return an empty landmarks list. Put every requirement you can into the schema, and check the values that matter. The handbook’s structured outputs page covers schema design and the cost of constrained decoding.

Function calling

A tool is a function that the model may ask your program to run: look up the weather, query a database, call an API. The model never runs anything itself. It writes the function’s name and arguments, your code runs the function, and the result goes back into the conversation. A tool call is therefore a structured output whose schema is “one of these functions, with valid arguments for it”.

Here are two tools, each a Python function plus a schema for its arguments:

Show get_weather, convert_currency, arguments 45 lines 
RATES = {"USD": 1.0, "EUR": 0.92, "JPY": 151.0}


def get_weather(city: str) -> dict[str, object]:
    return {
        "city": city,
        "temperature_c": 18,
        "sky": "light rain",
    }  # a stand-in for a weather API


def convert_currency(
    amount: float, source: str, target: str
) -> dict[str, object]:
    return {
        "amount": round(amount / RATES[source] * RATES[target], 2),
        "currency": target,
    }


def arguments(**properties: dict[str, object]) -> dict[str, object]:
    return {
        "type": "object",
        "properties": properties,
        "required": list(properties),
        "additionalProperties": False,
    }


TOOLS = {
    "get_weather": (
        get_weather,
        "Gets the current weather in a city.",
        arguments(city={"type": "string"}),
    ),
    "convert_currency": (
        convert_currency,
        "Converts an amount between currencies.",
        arguments(
            amount={"type": "number"},
            source={"enum": list(RATES)},
            target={"enum": list(RATES)},
        ),
    ),
}

The schema for a call allows any one of the tools: anyOf lists one object per tool, each with its fixed name and its own arguments. enum limits the currencies to the ones RATES knows, so the model can’t ask for one the function would fail on. The system prompt tells the model what the tools do:

Show call_tool 44 lines 
call_schema = {
    "anyOf": [
        {
            "type": "object",
            "properties": {"name": {"const": name}, "arguments": args},
            "required": ["name", "arguments"],
            "additionalProperties": False,
        }
        for name, (_, _, args) in TOOLS.items()
    ]
}
system = (
    "You can call these functions. Reply with one call as JSON.\n"
    + "\n".join(
        f"- {name}: {description} Arguments: {json.dumps(args['properties'])}"
        for name, (_, description, args) in TOOLS.items()
    )
)


def call_tool(question: str) -> tuple[dict[str, object], dict[str, object]]:
    messages = [
        {"role": "system", "content": system},
        {"role": "user", "content": question},
    ]
    call = json.loads(
        chat(
            messages,
            response_format={
                "type": "json_schema",
                "json_schema": {"name": "call", "schema": call_schema},
            },
        )
    )
    function = TOOLS[call["name"]][0]
    return call, function(**call["arguments"])


for question in [
    "What's the weather in Tokyo?",
    "How much is 20 US dollars in euros?",
]:
    call, result = call_tool(question)
    print(f"{question}\n  call:   {call}\n  result: {result}")

What's the weather in Tokyo?
  call:   {'name': 'get_weather', 'arguments': {'city': 'Tokyo'}}
  result: {'city': 'Tokyo', 'temperature_c': 18, 'sky': 'light rain'}
How much is 20 US dollars in euros?
  call:   {'name': 'convert_currency', 'arguments': {'amount': 20, 'source': 'USD', 'target': 'EUR'}}
  result: {'amount': 18.4, 'currency': 'EUR'}

Every call parses and runs, since the grammar allows nothing else. Whether the model picks the right tool is up to the model; this one does on these questions.

The last step gives the result back so the model can answer. Here the result goes into the system prompt:

question = "Should I take an umbrella in Paris today?"
call, result = call_tool(question)
print("call:", call)
print(
    chat(
        [
            {
                "role": "system",
                "content": f"Current weather: {json.dumps(result)}",
            },
            {"role": "user", "content": question},
        ]
    )
)

call: {'name': 'get_weather', 'arguments': {'city': 'Paris'}}
As a traveler, it's essential to consider the weather forecast and potential weather-related issues before making a decision. Paris is a city known for its rain, and the city's weather can be unpredictable.

If you're planning to visit Paris in the morning, it's best to take an umbrella. The city's rain can be heavy and unpredictable, especially in the mornings. A rain umbrella can provide some protection from the rain, but it's not a guarantee.

If you're

server.terminate()
_ = server.wait()

A 135M-parameter model uses the result loosely. Larger models follow it much more closely, especially ones trained for function calling.

Those models also take tools through the tools parameter of the OpenAI API. Their chat templates describe the tools to the model in the format it was trained on, and the model writes calls in its own format. MAX parses those calls with a tool parser for each model family, such as Llama, Qwen, and DeepSeek, and returns them as message.tool_calls; tool_choice="required" forces a call. max serve --tool-parser <name> picks a different parser than the architecture’s default, and --tool-parser none turns parsing off. SmolLM2’s chat template has no place for tools, which is why this section built the protocol by hand. The mechanism is the same in both cases: the model writes a structured call, and your code runs it. The handbook’s function calling page covers the API and the agent loops built on it.

Exercises

  1. Remove Field(min_length=3, max_length=3) from City. What does the model return now?
  2. Add a third tool, get_time(city), and a question that needs it. Does the model choose it?
  3. max benchmark takes --response-format with a JSON schema. Start the server with --enable-structured-output, then benchmark it with and without one, as in Section 7.2. How much does constrained decoding cost per token? Compare TPOT: constrained requests stop at the end of the JSON, so their output lengths differ.

Next

Section 7.4 points the same client at state-of-the-art open models on Modular’s cloud.

7.4 Modular’s cloud

Everything in this book ran on your own machine: models of up to 0.6B parameters, with multi-device meshes simulated on a CPU. Serving state-of-the-art open models to real traffic takes more: the largest models span many GPUs, capacity has to follow demand, and someone has to respond when a node fails. Modular’s cloud is an inference service that runs those models on MAX, the framework you used in this book. This last section points your client code at it by changing the base URL, the API key, and the model name.

import os

from openai import OpenAI

from minillm.config import MODEL_ID
from minillm.server import start_max_serve

Two ways to call a hosted model

Modular’s cloud serves popular open models behind the same OpenAI-compatible API that the earlier sections of this chapter used:

  • Shared endpoints are billed per token, and their capacity scales automatically, down to zero when idle.
  • Dedicated endpoints reserve GPUs for you and are billed per minute, for consistent latency and guaranteed throughput.

The pricing page compares them.

The same client, a different URL

Every server in Chapter 7 spoke the OpenAI API, and so do Modular’s endpoints. Here is the client from Section 7.1 against a local server again:

server = start_max_serve(MODEL_ID, "--quantization-encoding", "float32")


def ask(client: OpenAI, model: str, question: str) -> str:
    completion = client.chat.completions.create(
        model=model,
        messages=[{"role": "user", "content": question}],
        max_tokens=60,
        temperature=0,
    )
    return completion.choices[0].message.content


local = OpenAI(base_url="http://localhost:8000/v1", api_key="unused")
print(ask(local, MODEL_ID, "What is the capital of France?"))
server.terminate()
_ = server.wait()

The capital of France is Paris.

To call a hosted model instead:

  1. Sign up at console.modular.com.
  2. Create an API key with model access on the API keys page, and set it as the MODULAR_API_KEY environment variable. In Colab, add it as a secret and copy it into os.environ.
  3. Pick a model on the endpoints page, and set its model ID as MODULAR_MODEL.

Only the base URL, the key, and the model name change. The cell skips the request when no key is set:

api_key = os.environ.get("MODULAR_API_KEY")
if api_key is None:
    print("Set MODULAR_API_KEY and MODULAR_MODEL to call a hosted model.")
else:
    hosted = OpenAI(base_url="https://api.modular.com/v1", api_key=api_key)
    print(
        ask(
            hosted,
            os.environ["MODULAR_MODEL"],
            "What is the capital of France?",
        )
    )

Set MODULAR_API_KEY and MODULAR_MODEL to call a hosted model.

The API is the same, so code written against it, such as the structured output in Section 7.3, works with hosted models that support the feature. max benchmark measures a hosted endpoint as it measured your local server. The mcloud backend sends the key from OPENAI_API_KEY. A hosted endpoint manages its own cache and doesn’t expose server metrics, so the command skips both. If the model ID isn’t a Hugging Face repo, add --tokenizer with the repo of its tokenizer:

OPENAI_API_KEY=$MODULAR_API_KEY max benchmark --backend mcloud \
  --base-url https://api.modular.com --endpoint /v1/chat/completions \
  --no-flush-prefix-cache --no-collect-server-stats \
  --model $MODULAR_MODEL \
  --dataset-name random --random-input-len 128 --random-output-len 32 \
  --num-prompts 32 --max-concurrency 8

Before you choose between shared and dedicated endpoints, benchmark with your real prompt and answer lengths; the latency targets from Section 7.2 apply unchanged. The handbook’s choosing the right model and calculating GPU memory pages help you choose a model and estimate the GPUs it needs.

What you built

You started this book with three lines of AutoPipeline. Since then you:

  • wrote SmolLM2 from tensors up, checked it against MAX’s implementation, and added sampling and a KV cache (Chapter 1);
  • sharded it across a device mesh (Chapter 2);
  • wrote a Mojo kernel and built a graph by hand (Chapter 3);
  • tested, benchmarked, and profiled it (Chapter 4);
  • built a serving engine with continuous batching, a paged KV cache, prefix caching, and speculative decoding (Chapter 5);
  • brought up Qwen3, Gemma 3, and a mixture of experts (Chapter 6);
  • served your own architecture, measured it, and constrained its output (Chapter 7).

A model takes the same path into MAX, at a larger size. To keep going:

  • Read MAX’s guide to model bring-up and port a model you care about.
  • Work through the Mojo GPU puzzles to write the kernels behind it.
  • Read the handbook for the ideas behind every chapter.
  • Sign up to run state-of-the-art open models on Modular’s cloud, or request a demo to talk about your workload.
